WARRINGTON UNION.—PERSONS willing • to CONIRACT for the supply of any of the under, mentioned articles from Lady Day to Michaelmas next. To abe delivered free of expense at tie Warrington Union Workhouse, or at the Industrial Schools, Pad- gate, at such times and in such quantities as the Board of guardians shall direct, are requested to send in TENDERS, properly sealed, endorsed, and free of expense, addressed to the Clerk of the Guardians, before four o'clock p.m. on TUESDAY, the 12th day of MARCH, 1901. The following are the articles for which TENDERS are required : Groceries and Provisions Shoes, Clogs and Slippers Beef and _ Mutton Glazing _ _ _ _

.. Joints for Officers Broshes Flour and Irish pin head meal Coffins • Loaves and Bread Beet Oakum rope for picking Straw and Potatoes Best Penmaenmawr Stone Drapery &e, for breaking purposes Cord and Twill Leather Coal, Black, and Gas Coke

Samples of any of the above may be seen on application to the Master of the Workhouse. The Coal and Slack supplied must oe to the satisfaction of the House and Schools Com- mittees. The Pit and quality of coal must be named la the Tender. Forms of tender and fell particulars as to the probable quantities of each article required may be obtained at the Clerk's Office. _ _

Each tender must be made upon the prescribed form and properly enclosed and endorsed with the name of the article tendered for. Each article tendered for must be of size material and quality shown at the Workhouse. No tenders for other articles will be accepted. Bach contractor must be pre- pared (if required) to enter into security for the due perform- ance of his contract. The Guardians do not bind themselves to accept the lowest or any tender. All contract accounts will be paid quarterly.—By order. JAMES CLOWNS SUTTON. Clerk to the Guardians. Bewsey Chambers, Warrington, February 26th, 1901.

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAR 1\ ELLIE, The burnt straw hats which some milliners endeavour to make their customers believe to be mournin* ar certaiuly not so, even though trimmed with clack. Black violets and black roses make pretty and effective trimmings, nest- ling in among chiffon and taffetas. The favourite all-round shape, which is worn tilted well forward over the face, as indeed most of the better hats are, is stylish wear for mourning, with a plain tailor costume, simply trimmed with a soft silk scarf, notted at one side into a large careless bow. Toques of black gauze with aigrettes of small ostrich feathers, or of dull silk trimmed with jet- centred flowers, are very fashionable, as are those of crape-straw, twisted into bows and loops and mingled with dull silk and stiff black wings. Thco there are large hats of soft black felt with drooping brims, trimmed with glace silk bows and bang feathers, or of chip with a big ruche of silk gauze round the low crown, a couple of jet buckles in front, and strings of the gauze tying under the chin with long ends. Later on velvet bats will be .again in favour, while the sombreness of all millinery will be relieved with clusters of ondlei white and mauve flowers

The latest evening bodices have a stiff, straight, very pointed stomacher in the centre, which gives a flat, hard look to the figure, and, of course, the straight-fronted corset underneath is a necessity. These stomachers are encrusted with jewelled em- broidery, or covered with rich lace, and the lace fichu round the décolletage seems to be the natural accompaniment. Elbow sleeves with full lace ruffles will be the chief wear for both day and evening gowns, as the weather grows warmer. Shorter sleeves mean the use of long mousquetaire ewes, which give such a graceful effect ruffled up the arm to the elbow. Taffetas shirts, tucked and ribbed perpendicularly from shoulder to waist, asomery suitable for morning wear, but on- teas theAaffetas be of a very good, soft quality, it isiaptt split.and crack where it goes into folds— whilst.enraia often frays itself cut at the seams—a good make of China silk is best for wear. The mew bolero shirt is a very original model, both shistiand simulated bolero being cut with over- =stitched pleats. A bolero of guipure or cloth is much worn in the house over a et-shirt, by those who feel the blouse pure ali,l simple to be somewhat too youthful in style.

At the present time, the materials most in favour for outdoor costumes are vicun frieze serge and dull smooth clo:11, and tiley are, as a rule, made up very plainly with stitched, braided or corded skirts and reefer or blouse coats, faced with mourning silk. Large full boas of black gauze or chiffon are much worn, although sable and sealskin are both admissible furs for mourning wear. as well as cancel, astrachan and black fox. Cream or white Iwo collars are temporarily out of favour, but heavy black guipure lace is used a good deal, and jet, in both the dull and the cut varieties, is usedas a trimming, especially for indoor dresses. These latter are being made of all kinds of soft, black woollen materials, especially cashmere and thin cloth, and many models have full vests of blackisoft silk or gannyfinely tucked or smocked, while others are brightened by vests of white chiffon sometimes strapped down with very narrow bands of black glace silk and tiny jet buckles. Long swathed sashes of soft silk make a nice finish to these house-gowns, but shaped and, boned belts of glace are quite as much in vogue, a.ndiare less expensive. Deep mauve and purple g see blouses, trimmed with black lace appliques or jetted pas- sementerie, are not infrequently worn with black skirts indoors, but with walking-costumes all. black blouses are more correct, according to pre- vailing ideas.

Poplin is very well worn, both for day and evening and it is a material to which pathetic interest attaches now, in view of the liking our late Queen had for this fabric and the support she gave to Irish manufactures generally. The demand for ready-made skirts and blouses has been enormous, for there is not that discrepancy between a black blouse and skirt that there is between a coloured one, and the combination admits of some very elegant effects. The prettiest blouses are those simple, soft silk slips, fastening down fhe back, with transparent inlet yokes and collars of black lace or guipure ; these are confined at the waist by a broad corselet belt or a narrow ribbon, as the shape of the figure demands. WOMEN'S CHAT.

The King sod Queen have always taken the greatest interest in the gardens and home farms attached to the Royal residetces. Queen Alexandra is a devoted lover of flowers, and the beautiful gardens at Sandringham, where the early spring flowers may be seen at their best. have long been her Majesty's especial care. There is also an extensive home-farm at Sandringham, where, as also at Windsor, the Royal herds of pure bred cattle,are among the finest in the world. Fortunately, both the King, and the Duke of Cornwall and York take much the same practical interest in agriculture. as did the late Prince Consort, who established the famous Shaw, and Flemish Farms at Windsor,about 50 or 60 years ago. This interest in farming is also shared by Princess Henry of Battenberg to whom the stock on the large home farm at Osborne, has been left, by the late Queen. After the death of George 111, royalty ceased to take any practical interest in agriculture, until Prince Con- sort took it up, and it is satisfactory that this will be continued, as it is announced that all the Royal farms at Osborne, Windsor, and at Abergeldie Maine, near Balmoral, will be kept on by the King as hitherto. There could hardly be a more sad and eorrowtul journey than that of the King to visit his widowed and tnvalid sister, the Empress Frederick, at Cronberg. The , accounts of Her Majesty"s illness vary at times, but all agree that the nature of the disease precludes any hope of recovery. Then the great sorrow which bas fallen upon the Royal Family, adds a still more mournful note to this sad end hopeless outlook. Altogether the Em- press Frederick seems to have had more than her share of the troubles which are the common let of humanity. Never very cordially received in Germany, she was en- gaged in one long struggl , with Bismarck, and many discourtesies embittered her married life. Then, just when he teethed them most, she lost the love and eup- port of her husband under the saddest possible circum- stances. Many sad memories must darken these closing days. and one can only feel the greatest sympathy for the Empress Frederick and her family. Friedrichshof, where the Empress Frederick lies, is indeed a treasure house of curiosities, and objets dart collected by Her Majesty. During the early days of widowhoodehis hobby helped thelEmpress through many a sad hour, and even now her interest in an ancient vase or piece of tapestry can frequently be aroused. The magnificent library at Friedrichshof contains a valuable collection of books, and here much of Her Majesty's time was spent until very recently. Like her mother, our late beloved Queen, the Empress Frederick barever devoted little time to novel reading, preferring above all others, historical, theological, or biographical writ ks. Of Queen Victoria's daughters, only one, the Emnresa Frederick is entitled to wear uniform—Her Majesty being Colonel-in-Chief of the 2ad Prussian Hussars, also commander of a regiment of German Fusiliers. Of the late Queen's daughters-in-law. three out of the four can don uniform if they please. Queen Alexandra is chief of a Dar ish cavalry corps, the Duchess of Saxe-Coburg- Gotha is Lead of a regiment of Russian Dragoons, and Colonel of a German Grenadier regiment, while the Duchess of Connaught is the nominal commander of the regiment cf infantry which was formerly captained by her illustrious father, Prince Frederick Charles of Pruse,a. Thete appointments are purely honorary ones of course, but some exalted women Colonels there are who have actually " seen service." Among them is the ex. Queen Marie of Naples, the heroine of the siege or Gaeta, the last stronghold in Italy of the ill-fated Emir- bonS. King Edward has always taken an active interest in the many attempts in karliament to remove the restric- tions upon marriage with a deceased wife's sister, and it is believed that there is now a good prospect of such a measure being passed into law this Session. The long struggle between the opponents and supporters of the Deceased Wife's Sister Bill, has been continued with varying fortune, but it is confidently expected that it will be finally decided in its favour this year. As a rule the Bishops have defeated these endeavours in the past though two years ago,such a Bill was passed in the House of Lords, when however it was too late for the Hews of Commons to take it up that Session. The present Bill, which would be retrcepective in its opera- tion, is In charge of Sir W Gurdon, and it has sr cured a favourable place for early discussion in the House of Commons. Should it receive the expected measure of support in that Chamber, there is little doubt that the House of Lords will consent to its being placed on the statute book in the first Parliament of the King. Cencernine the many taxes imposed by Society, the wedding present tax cacnot be said to rank among the least, and not a few folk have reason to be grateful for the fact that the present lull in weddings will as any rate last throughout Lent. There are a few fashionab e functions of this description arranged to take place after Easter has come and gone, but the rush is over, and Society folk, who have been called upon heavily of late, have for thi• year, weathered the worst of the wedding " present" storm. The shops are very busy with preparations for the Easter season, which, under ordinary circumstances, is the time of the year when they are seen at their best and brightest. All appearances go to show that trade will not suffer PO much as was expected. There is the necessary abandonment of many society entertainments, but the frequent appearance of the King and Queen in London tends to promote public activity, and there is a general disposition to zesnend to this encourage- ment. The necessity for introducing variety into the monotony of black, has brought to light much hidden talent, and it is positively marvellous, what a diversity is sow possible with the one sable hue only at our disposal. As the spring advances white and black will be the wear, and so soon as summer is with us, white unrelieved promises to become almost universal wear. At present there is little new to chronicle concerning skirts. They still fit perfectly over the hips, with a flat 1 box pleat at the b•ck, springing out below the knees into very wide full folds, which fall about the feet. Much has been said of late abeut the disappearance from the skirt fashionable of the shaped flounce., but, as a matter of fact, this form of trimmings was never in greater favour with leading dressmakers than at the moment. Usually they are deeper at the back than in the front, but the very newest skirts show a flounce of the same depth, the whole way round. *Accordian kilted turn s of black glace, edged with fine black lace, are taking the place of the fur neckwear of vied), together with the weather which has long made it a necessity, we are a little weary- Extra smart creations hay an inner ruffling of kilted tulle, which softens, and tones down the slight harshness of the glace These are likely to prove more lasting and substantial than rufli.s built of chiffon or tulle, especially when we take into consideration that the March winds lie before us. Ends of rather wide glace ribbon should reach well to the knees.

As a rule, the many attempts to improve upon the old-fashioned style of mourning envelope have not been a success. They have usually resulted in some device equally hideous, but this can scarcely he said of a novelty which has been produced to meet the large de- mand for mnurnirg stationery, in consequence of the death of the Queen. This consists of a small black bordered square in the corner, where ths stamp is placed. so I hat it appears with a mourning band round it. If such things are necessary, this is a diqtinct im- provement upon the old style of mourning envelope, with its unsightly blacie edges and seams. "MADGE."

(Conright.)

RUBY LOVEDAY.

BY SILAS K. HOCKING, Author of "The Strange Adventures of Israel Pendray," "The Day of Recompense," "God's Outcast," "In Spite of Fate," "Her Benny, " tax.

CHAPTER XVII.

A FAMILY COUNCIL. _ "We must not play the coward, Even though gain should lie that way."

It was evening and nearly dark when he reached Endilloe. The day had been one of the longest he could remember. So impatient was he to•reach home that he scarcely noticed the green mantle of spring with which nature was clothing the hills and fields. Several times he tried to lose himself in the paps of a stirring novel that he brought with him. But reading was altogether out of the question. Nothing would have suited him so well as being stoker on the engine. At last! at last!. The train had scarcely stopped when he was out on the platform. He had seen his father and was by his side in it moment. "My boy, you look pale and thin," John said, with a pathetic shake in his voice.: "I have been working hard," was the quick reply; "but how are you and the others ? " We are all very well; that is, we are very well in health," was the somewhat dubious answer; and then Justin's luggage claimed immediate attention. In a few minutes they were driving along the quiet lanes in the direction of Endißoe. .Justin's heart was too full for speech. The very smell of the dear homeland was like a benediction to him., The breezy April day was dying swiftly in the west, the outlines of the hills were growing vague and indistinct.

"Everything looks very natural, Father," he said at length, trying to keep out of his voice all trace of emotion. "Well, it's getting too dark to see stitch, my boy," was the reply. "But things don't change much down this way—at least, they don't in appearance." Justin was quick to note the qualifying note in his father's words, but decided to wait for explanations. • Dorothy was at the garden gate when the trap. drove up, and greeted her brother with a silent kiss. She could not trust herself to speak just then. At the open door stood the mother=a veritable Martha in her own house, cumbered about much serving. She kissed her son on both cheeks and then held his face between her hands and looked at him in silence.

There was an appetising supper ready to be put on the table, and in a few minutes the little family circle was complete once more. Justin gave a long sigh when his father came in and took his seat and the door was shut.

"Oh, it is lovely to be home again," he said. "One never knows how nice home is till one goes away." "We have missed you terribly, Justin," Dorothy answered, without looking at him. "And it seems years ago since you went away," said the mother.

John looked up as though he meant to say some- thing and then thought better of it, and proceeded to attack his supper. For awhile there was silence. Then Mrs. rentyre looked up and said: "And hare you come home for good, Justin ? " "I hope so, mother." "But it will mean a great loss to you ?* Dorothy questioned. „_ _ _

"That remains to be seen," he said, with a laugh. "And then you know, Dorothy, money is not everything." .. "And London is a terribly dear place tolive Mrs. Pentyre interposed. "There is no doubt about that, mother, but let's not talk about London now. What about Endilloe ? Have there been any fresh developments lately ? " John laid down his knife and fork and looked troubled. "I thought we wouldn't talk about it," he said, "till we'd finished our supper." "Then you have some fresh news ? " he questioned. "Yes Justin, we have, but—"

"Oh,' never mind th,e 'but,'" he interrupted, with a laugh. "Let's have it at once. I can assure you I'm not going to let it spoil my appetite." "Well, then, Justin, I'm afraid we shall have to leave here. Bice was round here again yesterday—" "Confound him!" "Yes, we may confound him as much as we like, but I'm afraid he's got the whip-hand of us." _

"Because we've no papers, Justin." "But what title has Lord Tregeigle ? " "Well, Bice says that he's absolute title, and that I and my father before me and his father and his—have all been living here on sufferance." "Which statement you asked him to prove ? " "I did; and he produced a big map, coloured red— " Which embraced Endilloe, of course ? " "It did, my son." • Justin laughed. "It'll take a good:deal of bluff of that kind US affect me," he said.

"What do you mean by bluff ? " Dorothy questioned. "I mean, Dorothy, that Bice and his master expect, no doubt., that if they only bluster, and shew fight, and threaten, and make assertions loud enough, that we shall yield, -and they will be able to take undisturbed possession."

"But what can a poor man do" John inter- posed, pathetically, "when a great landlord drops down upon him in that way? A big magnate like Tregeagle has all the machinery of the nation at his back." _ _

"I know all the odds are in his favour," Justin answered; "nevertheless, it would be cowardly to yield without a struggle. You are now about the only freeholder left in the parish, and Tregeagle wants to crush you out as he has crushed out all the others. But there's going to be a fight for it this time."

"I would not mind fighting," John said, sadly, "if any good was likely to come out of it." "No good can come of quietly yielding," Justin said, with spirit. "I don't know about that," John replied. "Bice says that if I let the thing go without making a fuss, there is a chance that,' may be allowed to have the farm again at the same rent." "Did he say that?" "Well, those are not his exact words, but he hinted at it plain enough. -On the other hand, if I do resist, I shall be turned out of the farm, and in the end I fear shall lose Endilloe into the bargain." "Oh, I see. Well, they are playing a pretty_ deep game; but it isn't going to answer this time." John shook his head.

- "No, father. You have sent for me and I have come home, but I have not.corue home to yield up my life at the bidding of the agent of Lord Treg- sagle. I have come home to fight this thing out and I intend to do it to the last drop of my blood." Dorothy's nostrils dilated and her eyes sparkled, but she did not say anything just then." ”But there is another side to the question, my son," John said, after a pause. "If Endilloe really does belong to Lord Tregeagle, as Mr. Bice says it does, why— . 'Please let us not discuss that if,' father," Rutin interrupted, hastily. "Endilloe has belonged to the Pentyres for generations." "But Lord Tregeagle is an honourable man, and be evidently believes that Endilloe belongs to him." "Very likely he does. Men of his class generally believe that everything belongs to them that they can lay hands on." - "Well, and when thingrare sifted to the bottom, it is generally found that they are right. They own nearly all the land of England." "Quite true," Justin said, with a cynical smile. "Well, look at Wintel•down Common as a case in point," John went on. "There were three hundred acres of it, and it had beet looked upon as common land for generations. Everybody that liked turned Out his cows and sheep and donkeys on it. But it was ehewn, in the end, that nobody had any right to do it. The land all the while belonged to Lord Tregeagle and Lord Pottyways and ten, years ago, as you may rentelober, they divided it equally between them and it is now let out in small farms which bring them in a good rent." "Exactly," Justin' U.))suetvd "and the same story may be tom in neagy every parish in England. A hundred years ago there was some common land in England that belonged to the people. There was a green in. every village where the children had a right to play. But that day is over. Nearly every acre of common land has been filched from the people and added to the estates of the rich." "No, do not say filched, ' Justin. You might to well say that they have stolen the land." "Exactly. Ido not mind which word you use, either will snit Inn." ' • "But they don't suit me, my son. You can't object to people claiming their rights." gi

"Rights, father?" he said, slowly and bitterly. "They have grown up—have been cradled in the belief, in fact, that they only have rights, and that the poor people have no rights at all. We have been robbed and oppressed for centuries." "No, no, that is wild Radical talk, and I hate It. I believe in the old ways, and the old laws, and the old constitution, and we are taught to render respect to our betters." "Oh, yes," Justin said, impatiently. "I will render respect to any man who is deserving of respect. But when a man tries to rob me, then I will Tesist him to the last gasp." "I don't blame you for feeling strongly," John .111 lied slowly. "I felt just as bitter myself when Id Bice first came sneaking round here. But when he assured me yesterday that Lord Tregeagle's title was good and that he'd been generous all these sears in not enforcing it, what could I do?" "You could have given him the lie to his face. As it is, he will assume that you have lost, or mislaid, the deeds, and so will become all the more arrogant inconsequence." _

"Oh, 1:ve1l, Justin, I'm not a fighting man, as you know, and I'm awfully glad you've come home, for I've been at my wits' end." "And will you leave the matter now in my hands "

"Well, Justin," and John shook his head slowly from side to side, "it's this way: you never would be a farmer, and there is nothing else that I can do. And if I'm turned (Alt from here, what's to become nf me?"

Justin dropped his head, and for some time ate in silence. His father seemed bent on giving away the whole case, and if he was going to remain in that mood, there was no use in attempting anything. He would be like a man fighting with his hands Lied.

Dorothy was watching him intently. She could read his face like a book, and she dreaded lest he should say that he would give up the whole conten- tion, and go away again. For herself, she believed in resisting to the last. The Pentyres in the past had been much too yielding, and consequently had lost nearly all their possessions. It was time that someone came who had strength to resist. She spoke up at last. "Are you forgetting those who will come after you, father ?" she inquired. John started and looked at her for a moment in silence.

"I'm afraid I have been forgetting," he said at length. "Yes, it's a very proper question." Justin's brow cleared at once, and he looked across the table at Dorothy and smiled. John did not speak again for some time, then he turned to Justin.

"Endilloe is as much yours as mine," he said, slowly. "I was forgetting that. And if you like to fight for it I'm not going to stand in the way. But I'm afraid it will be a losing game, my son. You see, we've no papers." "Possession is nine points of the law," Justin said, with a laugh, "and we'll bluff the tenth."

"Bluffing may do with a man your own size," aohn answered, pathetically. "But think of Lord Tregeagle's purse, and influence, and position. Besides, he has the whole House of Lords at his back."

"Yes, I know all that," Justin said, bitterly. "And I don't know that I have any real hope of winning. It is not often, it seems to me, that the Pentyres have been on the winning side; but at any rate there will be the satisfaction of resist- ing wrong and oppression. In a fight of this kind the poor man has scarcely a chance, I know. But he need not play the coward on that account. I would a thousand times rather die fighting for the right than yield to the wrong and live." "Very good, my son," John said, with a little sigh. "Then we shall have our answer ready when Mr. Bice calls to-morrow."

"Leave Mr. Bice to me," Justin said, smiling. "I have often ached to have a word with that gentleman." So the little family council settled the matter and waited rather impatiently for the coming day.

!CHAPTER XVIII'. PREPARING FOR ACTION. 'I have set my life upon a cast, And I will stand the hazard of the tie."

- Long before noon on the following day, every- body in St. lago knew that Justin was back again from London, and most people had a fairly shrewd guess as to the reasons that had brought him home. It was known that John Pentyre's "take" ran out at Midsummer, and that Mr. Bice had given the farmer no promise that it should be renewed. It had also got whispered abroad that Lord Tregeagle had been claiming, through his agent, Endilloe House with its gardens, orchard and twenty acres of meadow land, and that it was doubtful if John Pentyre had any legal title to the place. Nor was that all, it was believed that Lord Tregeagle was all-powerful in the county, and that it was useless for any poor man to pit his strength against him. Hence, if he had cast covetous eyes on John's little bit of freehold, that freehold would be his, and John Pentyre would resist and wriggle in vain.

Of course, everybody sympathised with John in this new calamity. There was something pathetic in the sight of this ancient and once nourishing family being reduced to the level of the common herd; but it had been the fate of nearly every small freeholder in the parish. For some reason or other the big estates had grown, and the small estates had dwindled, and the case of Endilloe was evidently going to be a repetition of several others. "I reckon Maister Justin be a corned home from up London Churchtown for to stop old Bice from gobbling up Endilloe," Amos Blue said to Nathan Hendy on the morning after Justin's return, "but he might as well try to stop the tide from coming in down to Poldula porth, that's what I do say." "I'm glad he's come home, all the same," Nathan replied. "It's wastin' a good life to live in thews big towns; the folks be that ignorant." "I don't know 'bout that," Amos said, reflec- tively. "Ef it be as you say, why be folks always crackire up London and' the folks as live there?" "Pure ignorance," said Nathan, with energy. "They don't know no better, that's the reason. Look at old Bice; he ain't never lived in no big town, and see what he knows. He could tie knots in any Lohdoner as ever was. He can make blacl- white wi' just winkin', an' can write lies on paper as looks so much like truth that nobody could tell the difference. He means to gobble up Endilloe, an' I agree wi' you, Amos, as he's going to do it." "But if the =Aster have the papers all in proper order? " questioned Amos, as if anxious to hope even against hope. "I've thought of that too," said Nathan, "but, lor, when old Bice makes up his mind to a thing it's all up. You see, he's Lord Tregeagle at his back and all the rest of the big wigs, an' they all hang together like a string of onions." "But it's hard on the poor folks," said Amos. "Aye, but it's our lot," Nathan said, with a sigh..-'Providence, I suppose, moaned it to be so, so 'taint no use complainin ." "I don't know," said Amos. "If I'd anything to fight for—which I ain't—l do believe as how I'd kick out to the last. An' I hope as how Mr. Justin 'll shew fight in this case." "I'm afraid as how living up in that big town have spoilt 'in," Nathan remarked, with a grunt, and walked away. Meanwhile, Justin and Dorothy were discussing the situation over a late breakfast. Mrs. Pentyre was bury in the dairy, and John was superintending affairs on the farm.

"0 Sustin," Dorothy had said, "I cannot tell you how delighted I am to see you home again, but I have been wondering all the night what you will do now you are at home."

"Neva- fear, Dorothy," he answered, brightl:r. "I shall be able to get my bread somehow.. But my first business will be to find the Endilloe deeds and checkmate old Bice."

"And suppOse you fail. Suppose the deeds are irretrievably lost. What then ? Father and mother have searched everywhere, and they have both given up hope." "Of course, if we are ejected from Endilloe, wo shall have to find some other place." "It will nearly break father's heart." "That is all the more reason why I should stand by him. We 'night take a smaller place than this, and the two of us might do the work." "But you hate farming, Justin."

"No' It is the conditign. s under which we groan that I hate, though I admit I should never take up farming from choice." "I wish I knew how I could earn something," Dorothy said, with a sigh. "There seems no chance for women in this world."

"Unless they go out as domestic servants," Justin answered, with a laugh. "Then they have it all their own way." "Do you think I might succeed in that line?" Dorothy questioned, looking up at him. "You might if you could pocket the family pride." "Would you like me to make the attempt?" "No, Dorothy, while I am able to keep a roof over my head there will always In a home tor you." She turned away her eyes from him and looked out el the window, and he fancied he saw the

glisten of a tear-drop on her lids. For awhile neither spoke again. Then he said, abruptly: "Do you ever hear from Tom Pendarvis now? She started, and a hot blush swept over her face. "No, Justin. Why do you ask?" "I hardly know," he said, hesitatingly. "Curiosity, I expect." "I keep hoping every day," she replied, the colour fading slowly from her face, "that I shall hear from him." "Then you believe in him still? " "I have had no reason for doubting him," was the quick reply. "But it is more than three years ago since he went away." "If he keeps silence for thrice three years I shall still believe in him. Justin sighed, but he asked Me more questions just then. He had no wish to cast a single shadow across Dorotby's hope. Let her cherish it as long as she coeld. His had gone out in darkness an too soon. It seemed only yesterday since he had sat in that very room, and built the most romantic castles that anyone had ever seen. He would go to London. He would seize Dame Fortune by the hand directly on his arrival. He would rise more. • rapidly in the commercial world than Dick Whittington of olck Be would shew not only his, Cornish friends, but all London, what energy, and honesty, and enterprise could accomplish, and then he would offer his hund and his fortune to Ruby Loveday, and she would crown his life with her love to the end of his days. He was glad now that he had never taken anyone into his confidence. To what an inglorious end his hope and striving had come. How miserably all his schemes had failed. How absolutely wasted had been the treasure of his love. No doubt a similar fate was in store for Dorothy, but he would not tell her so. Let her go on loving and hoping. Hope enriched, though it went out in darkness, and Love ennobled the life though it might never be returned. It was not by being loved that the heart was purified,, but by loving. Breakfast finished, Justin put on his hat and went out into the spring sunshine. How wide the world seemed after the London streets. How sweet and strong the air that blew in from the sea. How absolutely unfettered he felt. He wanted to run and jump and shout, and had he been certain that no one would have seen or heard him, he would' have done so. Down in ODO of the fields- he came across Amos digging out a ditch. Amore straightened his back with unusual alacrity and waited for Justin to• come up. "I be purty an' glad to see 'ee, Meister Justin," he said, eagerly, breaking in upon Justin's friendly "Good-morning." "Terrible glad I be, anduthere's no denyin' it. I only wish I could see our Dan'l 'ome from the same place." "Daniel is still in London, is he?" Justin questioned, with a smile. "Oah, iss. Dedn'ee never see 'im all the time you was up in that there town, now? " "No, I never once caught sight of him. I looked out for him, of course." "'Tee terrible strange," Amos said, reflectively. "'Ome here to St. lago people do get to knaw each other terrible quick." "But you see London is a much bigger place." "Iss, us, so they do say. But ded 'ee ever ax for our Dan'l when you was up there?" "I am afraid asking would not have helped me much," Justin said, with a smile. "Well, Nathan do say that they be terrible ignorant folk up there an' don't know nothing; an' • if that be so, I don't s'pose tess much use aria'." "Well, you know, Amos, it isn't possible for people to know everything." "No, I s'pose not. Nathan do say that it's a pity people have to live in them ignorant places. I do wish our Dan'l would git out of it." "And haven't you heard from him lately?" "Oah, no. It's months agone now. He said then as 'ow he was workin' on the parish roads under the Corporation. I'm sorry he's workin' under anything. I always think it's healthier, Meister Justin, to work up in the open air." And Amos, who was a conscientious man, thinking he had wasted quite enough time, turned again to his work. Mr. Bice did not shew his face that day, nor, indeed, for several days after. He knew, of course, that Justin had come home from London, and con- cluded that his father had sent for him. On the whole he was sorry, for Justin was made of much less pliable material than his father and he was anxious to settle the little matter he had in hand. with the least possible amount of friction; and, indeed, he had assured his master that the thing would be done so quietly that no one would know anything about it until it was an accomplished fact. A week before he had felt certain that he had accomplished his object. He had placed before John Pentyre as delicately as he could the alterna- tive. His lease of the farm might be renewed for fourteen years at the same rental if— What that "if" was he allowed John to infer. To his great satisfaction John appeared inclined to swallow the bait. Now, however, Justin had come upon the scene, and that might mean further arguments and much more drastic measures. He knew nothing about Justin personally, but he had been told that he was a young fellow of strong will and of unyielding obstinacy, and it was very likely he would incite his father to resist .even when it could be shewn that resistance was useless. Mr. Bice was not a man who was much troubled with scruples. He had one aim in life—to serve his master well ; for by serving him he indirectly served himself. Lord Tregeagle, like more than one of his ancestors, had set his heart on painting the map of St. lago all one colour. The sight of this map, with little dabs of other colour paints, made him irritable, as it had made his father and grandfather, irritable before him.. Those small freeholders were interlopers and intruders whose presence was offensive, and who were to be got rid. of as quickly as possible. By various methods, known only to the great and rich and strong, most of them had been got rid of. John Pentyre was the last of any conse- quence that remained, and how he was to be removed was for a long time a puzzle. Mr. Bice, however, had staked his reputation on the accomplishment of the task, and for many years had been exercising his wits in all directions. At last, however, by various little tricks and manceu- vres, he had fully convinced himself that if John Pentyre had ever possessed the deeds of Endilloe he had lost them. Having come to that conclusion he fancied that he would be quite safe in playing a game of bluff, and up to now the results had been quite satis- factory. John Pentyre had fallen into the trap in the most simple and head-over-heels fashion. "We are quite safe, my lord," Mr. Bice said to his master one evening. "Pentyre rode into Trel- ford yesterday to consult his lawyer, and to-day, by Jove! he's as meek as Moses." "Is that so ? " "I assure you he's quite a different man. A few days ago he stood on his dignity and nearly ordered me off the premises. To-day all the fight has gone out of him. Hence, the thing is as plain as a pikestaff. He believed that Nankivel had the deeds and discovered that he hadn't, and so he sees that he may as well give in quietly." "Of course," answered Lord Tregeagle. "It's absurd, on the face of it, to suppose that he had any real title to the place. It's hardly likely that the farm would be ours without the house and tenements." "That's quite true, my lord ; so we'll colour that part of the map red without further delay." Nevertheless, Mr. Bice was by no means pleased when he heard that John Pentyre's son had come home. At first he hoped that it was only for a brief visit and that in a few days he would be off again, in which case he would keep away from Endilloe until lie had taken his departure. Before a week was over he discovered that Justin had come home for good. he reflected, rubbing his chin between his forefinger and thumb. "I shall have to face it out with both of them. Well, it can't be helped, and the sooner it: is over the.better." So on the following morning he made straight for Endilloe, walked boldly up the garden to the front door and gave a loud knock. 1 LONDON'S FAMOUS CORPORATION

PLATE. " No city in the world, let alone in England, can com- pete with London in the beauty, quantiry, and wealth of the gold and silver plate belonging to it. Corporae tion," writes G A Wade in the " Windsor Magazine." "Even the keepers of it are not able to say off-hand how many piece 3 there are, uor what is the estimated value cif tl.e whole. But those who have been privileged to see it spread out in all its glory at a Royal banquet in the Mansion House, or packed away in the immensely strong rooms with iron doors below the ground floors nf the Lord Mayor's official residence, where it is kept when not in actual service can alone form any idea of the brave display it makes beneath the glare of the electric light. It would be impossible te cl,scribe here one tenth of this famous plate, wh‘cla corn- prises every kind of gold and silver table ornament or useful adjunct, from massive gad ewer and basins to little gold sruff-boxes and finger-glasses. The oldest piece of plate now possessed by the City Corporation is what is termed the Christopher Cup,' which was given in 1662 by Robert Christopher, a few years before the Great Fire of London. Noteworthy amongst so many beautiful articles are three large gold 2agons, three rose. water dishes and ewers, and a very large antique- shaped vase, which weighs 235 ounces and is 22 inches high. Many of the pieces of plate bear on them the motto of the City, ''Domine Diego Nos," and inscriptions narrating the circumstances under which they were presented to the Mansion House. One interesting point about this celebrated collection is the fact that it is ever on the increase, as each Lord Mayor, on the conclusion of his year of office, is ex- pected to add a piece of plate of silver or gold, and of substantial value, to the existing amount. The • Irish Cup 'and salver are extremely valuable, and were a pre- sent from the Irish Society to the Right Hon. Henry Singleton, Esq , Lord Chief Justiee of Ireland, for eminent services bestowed in adjusting long-standing disputes between the two cities of 'London and London- derry. With his usual modesty the judge declined to receive the present, though he acknowledged hand- semely how touched he felt by its offer, The fine gift was therefore sent to London so he used by the Lord Mayor and Corporation in perpetuity, and bears an inscription to that effect, with the date 1741. Since that time it has been several times repaired and regilded., It has the arms of London and Londonderry engraven on it side by side. Another exceptionally fine niece of plate is the gift of the mayors of Belgian municipalities to Sir Potydore de Keyser, who came of en old Belgian family, on his being elected Lord Mayor of London. It is a superb piece of work and stands about twenty inches high, its lid being surrounded with real turquoise and emeralds in a wide circle, and a still wider circle of the same precious gems surrounding the base of the cup. There is a beautifully modelled figure of a knight OR the top of the lip. The Belgian mayors have been more than once invited over here to London, and have been enter- tained right royally in commemoration of their kindness in making such a presentation."

BRABAZON SCHEME AT WORK.

Sale of Workhouse Inmates' Work. The work which has been done by the aged and infirm inmates of the Warrington Workhouse under the Bra- bazin Employment Scheme was offered for sale on Tees- d4Y afternoon in the Council Chamber of the Town Dail. The hall had been lent for the occasion by the Mayor (Alderman Roberts). There Were only two Guardians present—Mrs Eaelsten and the Rev T R Matthews—owing to the meetings of the Central Poor Law C niference and the Padgate Schools Committee. The Rev E R Tarbuck (chaplain of the Workhouse,) pre- . •il,al at the opening proceedings and amongst those also present were the Revs Lei T Dar well sal John Yonge. fhe goods offered for sale were arranged on a table in the centre of the room, and included rugs, screens, nick. flacks and other artieles. Considerable skill had been shown in the work and the ladies who had. taugh t the Inmates must have given much painstaking care to the work. Mrs. EDELSTEN read the following report :—There is very little that is fresh to tell you of the work of the year. Perhaps not quite so much as last year naq done, but, as you all know, we were moving ici4 beautiful new infirmary, and things were rather ali for a tim'. ANlEMlA.—Poorness of blood. Pull down the eyelid: if pale and bloodshot, it is a sign of Anwmia. Lack of colour in the lips and gums is another sign; also pallor oi the face; indigestion, feeble appetite, palpitation and shortness of breath after slight exertion, languor, "laziness," low spirits. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have cured thousands of severe cases. Anemia, if not checked, may become Consumption.

BILIOUSNESS.—A cull pain in the head, aggravated by stooping forward; yellow skin; yellow- ness in the whites of the eyes; nausea; shivering and goose-flesh. Often a result of indiscretions in diet or lack of out-door exercise. To cure it, use opening medicine, followed by a course of

Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, but do not commence the latter until the bowels have been freely moved.

RHEUMATISM.—Severe pain in the joints or in the muscles—pain aggravated by cold. It often arises from exposure to cold and damp, or from a wetting. Rheumatism may develop into Rheumatic Fever, and if not checked will occur again and again every winter.

===== 0002601_19010301_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
Dr. Williams' Pink Pius

have, perhaps, cured more cases of Rheumatism than of any other disease.

IN DIGESTION.--Pain in the stomach; fulness or distention after meals; wind on the stomach; hiccough, eructation, sickness, bile, distaste for food, a yellow tongue, bad complexion, bad taste in the mouth. These things arise from Indigestion, some- times called by its medical name Dyspepsia; they have been cured over and over again by

===== 0002601_19010301_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
Dr. Williams' Pink Pills.

Do not fly to purgatives, which only afford tem- porary relief.

INFLUENZA.—Intense or dull aching in the back and limbs; cold in the head and lungs; fever; debility; afterwards great prostration and depression of the heart, with violent headaches, and melancholy I hopeless view of life. If neglected, Rheumatic Fever or Decline may supervene. These many After-effects of Influenza are serious; in countless cases

Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have proved a safeguard and a restorative of the greatest value.

CONSUMPTION.—A wasting disease of the lungs. The patient loses weight ; spits blood; is weak, languid and pale; has slight feverishness, shortness of breath; pain in the chest; and profuse perspiration, especially at night. This disease is commonly regarded as incurable, but it has been frequently cured by 4

Dr. Williams' Pink Pills when taken in time. Consumption, when neglected or treated by ordinary medicine, invariably causes death.

ECZEMA.—An eruption of vessels or pimples on the skin, without fever; irritation, itching, and suppuration. Outward applications are of little use: the blood needs attention.

===== 0002601_19010301_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
Dr. Williams' Pink Pills,

though not a purgative, purify the blood, and give it richness and colour, causing all skin troubles to disappear and give place to a clear, smooth., healthy complexion. Use an aperient before commencing the pills.

HEART TROUBLE may show itself in two ways, both of which have been cured by

Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. The pulse may be slow and feeble; there may be intermission of a beat occasionally ; fainting, giddiness, and sick feeling. The other form of heart disease is manifested by the pulse being too quick; violent and throbbing pain in the head (especially after a slight climb or walking upstairs) ; noises in the ears.

HYSTERIA.—A nervous disease especially affecting young women; the unfortunate sufferer has uncontrollable fits of laughter and crying, accompanied by convulsions, choking sensation or fainting, and causing excessive weakness. The attack is often periodical, or may be brought on by a fright or mental agitation. As in nearly all the disorders peculiar to women,

Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have effected a cure over and over again.

SCIATICA.— Acute darting pain a pain almost like hot needles—in the hip, down the back of the thigh, extending to the knee, and perhaps to the ankle. The course taken by the pain is often tender to the touch. This painful disorder usually follows debility or a disordered state of the blood.

Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have afforded prompt and permanent relief in numerous cases.

Fireman Drowned at Acton Grange. Mr. Coroner Dobson conducted an inquest at Walton, on Saturday, on the body of a man named Charles Murdock, who lived in Roscommon-street. Liverpool, which was recovered from the Ship Canal at Acton Orange on Friday morning. The deceased was a fireman on the steamship Invalid, of Glasgow. BILLIARDS.

The Champion at the Warrington Liberal Club.

The visit of Mr. II W Stsvenson, the English ) champion at billiards, aroused a great deal of in- terest in Warriugton on Saturday, and those people who had friends at the Liberal Club were prompt in securing tickets for the exhibition games which had been arranged. Special accommodation had, ' been provided in the large billiard room of the club and it was possible for over 300 people to have an I excellent view of the games played on No. 1 table. In the afternoon Mr. Stevenson had two opponents lin Dr. Smith and Mr. J Jennings, whom he gave a start of 400 points in a game of 800 up. Mr. Jenn- ings had the firot essay and be went on until the champion had aggregated 400 points when he gave place to Dr. Smith. Mr. Stevenson won, of course, his opponents totalling 701. They both played con - sistently if not at their best, and made some useful contributions. Mr. Jennings highest break was 10, and hr. Smith contributed 29 and 25. Mr Steven- son was evidently not quite at home on such a fast table, but after a few strokes he settled down to beautiful billiards and was frequently applauded. His beet efforts were 94, 68, 61, 52, 46 (twice) 41, 37, and 29.

lathe evening there was a large attendance, all the available room being crowded with interested spectators. Mr A. H Crosfield, who is a very expert amateur cueist, was the champion's opponent, and it was expected that a very good game would re- sult, as Ins amateur was receiving a start ol 350 points in SOO. The two players had a very hearty reception, as also had lit Grosfield's brother,Captain George, whose face bore the bronze of the South African sun. The game commenced quietly, the champion running up a pretty little break which showed that he had quite mastered the "strength " of the table and accommodated himself to the peculiarities of the cushions. Mr Crosfield was com- paratively idle for some time, and it soon became apparent that he was a long way from being in his best form. He made a number of good indi- vidual shots, but generally speaking be failed to show that command over the balls which his riiends know him to be capable of. Stevenson, on tile other hand, played capitally and scored at gnat pace all through the game, which he eventually won by 190 point.. lie varied his play very fre- quently, and gave the spectators an exhibition of his wonderful skill • and delicacy with the cue that delighted all. He plays the top-of-the- table game to perfection, and his masterly nursing of thu balls when in position was a treat. Occa- sionally Mr. Crostield " potted" his formidable oppauent and hilt him in difficulties, which he was no sooner in than out by some particularly" classy" stroke. The champion's beet breaks were 105 (which elicite.l a round of hearty applause), 63, 58, 56, 55, 44, 37 and 30 (twice). Mr. Orosfield compiled 28, 27, 16. 14, and numerous small contributions. It was a source of regret to all, and to none more than himself, that Mr, Crosfidd was not able to do him- self justice but, we suppose, even Mr. Stevenson has his `' Off " day. At the conclusion of the game the champion gave an exhibition of trick strokes, many of woic:, were marvellous in their conception and superb in execu- tion. Ihey made a fitting finale to au exhibition which was a source of intense enjoyment to all who witnessed it. We must not omit to mention that the onerous task of marking was undertaken by Mr W. Woods, who acquitted himself well.

WARRINGTON TRADES COUNCIL

Special Meeting. j

The Resignation of Mr. Malkin.

A special meeting of the IWarrington Trades! and L thQur Council Was held in the Druide Hall, Sankey- street, on Tuesday evening. The President (Mr 4 C Sinks) presided over a good attendance,

THE :STATE OF TRADE IN THE DISTRICT-REPORTS FROM

THE SOCIETIES REQUIRED.

Mr KELLY, &ere' ary of the Lancashire Federation of Trades and Labour Councils, attended the meeting and delivered a brief address. He explained that he had been a labour correspondent for the Board of Trade, for seven years and had to supply information as regarded the state of trades in the Maocheeter district, which in- eluded Warringten. The Board had not experienced much difficulty in oPtaining information but what was de- sired was that correspondents in different parts of the country should furnish more detailed information. Some trades might be busy in one district, and a hundred miles away they would probably be slack, so that if they said that a certain trade which extended throughout the three kingdoms was good, bad, or indifferent they might be speaking truthfully as regarded one district but it would not be correct as regarded another. The Board of Trade de- sired that the federation should deal with separate societies locally, and the Board would deal with them in the bulk. It was requested that secretaries of societies would send him information as to how many members were unemployed and the state of trade. Employers would not obtain any direct information from the reports published in the " Gaznte," and they would have no means of ascertaining how many men were un- employed in each particular town. Mr Kelly proceeded to refer to the value of a federation of Trades Councils and reviewed several important mattera which had lately been under consideration. The CHAIRMAN moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Kelly for his address. He remarked that although it was Mr. Kelly's duty to collect reports for the Board of Trade they might also take it that he had come to Wariington as a person associated with them in the Federation of Trades Councils of Lancashire. He expressed the hope that all societies would endeavour to supply the infor- mation that Mr. K.lly desired. Mr. POTTS seconded and the motion was heartily carried. MR. MALKIN'S RESIGNATION—THE COUNCIL'S REPRRSEN-

TATIVE ON VIE BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The SECR-ET—A.I2-Y-read a letter which had been received from Mr TB Malkin with reference to his resignation as a delegate to the Council and as the Council's represen- tative on the Board of Guardians. He said that his reason for resigning was not because he was tired of the work • on the contrary it had been a pleasant and enj )yable experience which he should never forget or regret for be had made many friends. He could not, however,afford the time and expense, for it had cost him a great deal of time and money. lii the second place he had too much to do, and he could not and would int hold a position in which he could not do his duty to satisfy himself and those whom be represented. He did not think the Iron- workers' Society was seuding trom the Council, and at the next meeting he would tiring he matter forward, and the members would no doubt elect other delegates to the Council. A resolution acceptiag Mr Malkln's resignation was pasted. The CHAIRMAN Said it now rested with the members to decide whether they should put forward a candidate for the vacancy on the Board of Guardians occasioned by Mr Malkin's resignation. Mr Pl:morons thought it was very necessary that the Council should be represented on the Board of Guardians It would be of benefit to Trades' Unionists in the matter of contracts. The CHAIRMAN thought it would be a wise CDUISS for them to approach both political parties and hold that their candidate should be elected without opposition, seeing that one of their members was vacating the seat.

nig SECRETARY: That action was taken tirree years ago and both political parties seemed quite willing to allow us to have a reptesentative on the Board of Guardians if they could select the representative. (Laughter.) Mr Wit.Lcox proposed that Mr Plinston be invited to contest Bewsey Ward at the forthcoming election. The CRAIRMAN In seconding said that if Mr. Plinston felt inclined Co go forward as a candidate, he should be most happy to give him hearty support. Mr. PLINITON said be was not one of those indi- viduals who sought honours in the Town Council or Board of Guardians but if no other name was submitted he would consider the matter and let them know his decision on Monday. If be was elected a member of the Board his desire would be to serve those unfortunate individuals called the workingmen of Warrington. (Hear, hear.) Mr. WlLLcoz's resolution was curled with acclamma- tion.

It wag decided that. if necessary, the Secretary shall call a special meeting for next Tuesday.

- THE ELECTION OF AUDITORS.--.THE COUNCIL SUPPORT MR.

FLANAGHAN.

Mr M FLANAGHAN, OHO Of the candidates at this elec- tion, wrote to the Zouncil asking for their support. He also attended the meeting and stated tho grounds of his candidature. He was contesting the election, he said, for the reason that he thought, in fact he was perfectly sure, that accountants were undesirable men for the position. Although professionally, they were able to hold the office, they were dependent only upon a certain section of the u'elic for support. AS a ratep tver he approved of indepsndent principles in municipal affairs. He had been a Trades' Unionist f r 25 years and bad fought the ctuse of 7rades' Unionism in almost every part of the country. He therefore thought he was justified in appealing to the Council for support. On the proposition of Mr WILCOX, seconded by Mr FARGIIER it was decided that the Council give Mr Flanaghan its hearty moral support. This was all the business.

The North Cheshire Hunt.

The North Cheshire Hunt on Saturday met at Appleton, the Earl of Enniskillen, master, being in charge. The home covers furnished a vixen, which after dodging about got to ground. The covers all along the countryside were tenantless until Whit- ley was reached. Here there were three foxes. The selected one was hunted slowly to Marbury,through Cogshral, and back to Marbury,where he baffled the pack. Dog Kennel Wood,Mrabury yielded another, which ran two;or three fields towards Northwich, and was lost. A similar result followed after the hunt of a Cobler Gone fox,which ran into the Arley country.

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT

COUNCIL. Monthly Meeting..

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural Dis- trict Council was held at Bewsey Chambers on Wednes- day afternoon, There were present :—Ntessrs J Ball (in the chair), J W Knowles, Houghton, Brooks, Dignum, Halton and Thornton with Dr Sephton (medical officer), F Kerfoot (inspector), B 8 Newcombe (surveyor), and S Sutton (assistant clerk). THE MANURE "NUISANCE" AT PENEETH.-A SUFFERING PARISHIONERS SUgGESTION.

The following letter was read trom Mr E Foster, clerk to the Peraketh Parish Council The enclosed is a copy of an anonymous letter handed in to my Council at their last meeting. I am instructed to Inform you that my Council endorse the statements in the letter. They also wish to know whether there is any classi- fication in your bye-laws as to the nature of manure which can be discharged at the Fiddlers' Ferry wharf. They think there ought to be powers to prevent such stuff being delivered in our midst." The anonymous letter referred Co read as follows : To the Penketh Parish Council. A suffering parish- ioner begs to recommend that a supply of tar barrels be conveniently placed on the wharf a,: Fidders' Ferry winch could be ignired as soon as the operations com- mence in dialoading offal. This might to some extent neutralize the offensive stench and render the locality a safer place to live in. Mrs. Posnett encountered the terrible smell on Friday 28th 11.cember last. Dr. Ormsby pronounces her subsequent illness tl be a poisoned throat The writer was cold by the stationmaster on Thursday December 27th, that he had been ill all morning through the smells. Mrs. Withinshaw felt them powerfully at her house and many others can testify the same. Should any plague break out in the place, it will be more than easy to accoant for it, and of course, except those who are compelled to live here, it will become non-residental. Your petitioner will ever pray &c Mr. KNOwLEs : I recommend the parishioners to live at Longford for a week and then they would have a smell. (Laughter.) Dr. SEPHTON remarked that the Sankey sub-registra- tion district was always the most unhealthy in the Council's district. There had been five deaths during the past mouth. The district included Burtonvvood• Mr THORNTON : You do not attribute it to this cause ? Dr. SEPHTON : Certainly not. Mr. HOUGHTON thought the Council should take no notice of an anonymous letter. Mr. KNOWLES remarked that the manure was taken down the canal free of charge. If it came by train the railway company's officials would not make any "row." Mr. Honernron : If they take notice of anonymous letters they must not expect respectable bodies like this to do anything. The Council decithed to let the communication lie on the table.

THE BATES. The Assistant CLERK submitted the estimates for the ensuing half year. He said that at the commencement Of the previous half year a rate of 5 tin the £ for high- way purposes and id in the £ for sanitary purposes (making 61 in the £) was made. Owing to the recent purchase of a new steam roller it was thought that another penn von the ensuing year's rate would be necessary. He was, however glad to say that on going into the books and looking at the balances he found that the Council was in such a favourable position that it would not ho necessary to levy an extra penny for the ensuing half year. Th 3 rate would therefore remain , Unaltered.

HEALTH OT THE DISTRICT.

Dr. SLtrapoN reported that during tbe month there had been 30 births and 10 deaths. The death-rate was equal to 99 per 1,000, azainst 11•6 the average of the iast three months, and 26 the corresponding period of last year. The following eases of infectious diseases were notified :—Diphtberia, 4 ; scarlet fever,2 ; erysipelas, 1.

HE DIDN'T EAT THE DONKEY.

Ah, thera's where it is, you see. It's not the work so much as the worry that knocks us out and sends u prowling about after drugs and doctors. But how are we going to stave off the worry ? You'll have to ask that question of a Wiser chap than I am ; it beats me.

" It was worry," Me. Salsbury says, " that told on my health—worry and anxiety over my business. I got into a very low and depressed condition ; the energy and snap seemed clean gone out of me. I was worried about this too—one worry on top of another• " So I did what anyboiy would, in that shape I went to see a doctor. He appearei bothered to tell what ailed m,?, and finally said he thougnt I was suffering from a weak heart, and gave me medicine on that theory. "It did me no good, however. and I only grew weaker and more nervous than I was before. I was very constipated, and my appetite kept dwindling away until there was hardly a rag of it left. I have heard of people starving from lack bf victuals, and here I was—starving from lack of desire for food and power to digest it. In the end it's all the same. "At last the doctor gave me some medicine to saliv- ate me. It made my bead, from the crown to below the eyes, quite black; and also my hands as far as the wrists. Icm assure you I was a sight to look at." Mercury probably. A dangerous thing to put inside the humaa body. A man who knew what he was talk- ing about once wrote a piece of poetry on this medicine. Here are the last four lines : "And when I must resign my breata, Pray let me die a natural death, And bid the wo:Id a long farewell Without one dose of calomel." So say we all of us.

" I got so weak I could hardly lift my bands," adds Mr Salsbury. " Then something helpful happened. A man threw into my house a pamphlet telling of Mother Seigel's Syrup. I read the book and sent for the Syrup, and on goisg to bed I took a big dose—away beyond the directions—a teaspoonful and a half. In the early morning any bowels were opened for the first time in a week.

"Then I felt hungry and told my wife I to ultl eat a donkey and half his bre afast, but I contented myself with a meal easier of digestion. After that I got my etre igth and spirits bask like hauling in a line. "When the doctor called again he said, ' My word ! but you are picking up.' I was, but the remedy was Mother Seigel's Syrup. After two bottles I was all right and tackled my business with a light heart. All this came to pass when I lived in Grimsby."—W G Sals- bury. Upgate Lmth, Lincs., December 19th, 1899. Mr Salsbury is now manager of Bellamy Bros. Mineral Waters Manufactory. Upgate, Louth, and io well known there.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. The Rev. E. R._ Tarpuck and the

"Feathers Hotel " Licence. A Suggestion to the Corporation.

During th;course of his sermon at St. Paul's Church on Sunday evoniag, the Rev. E. B. Tarbuck referred to the question of the re-building of the" Feathers' Hotel') Bridge street. The rev. gentleman remarked that it had been suggested that it was possibc for the Corporation to turn that licence without absolutely throwing it away, into a means of competition with other houses by Putting its management on a higher principle. He hoped that if tbe Corporation decided to rebuild the Feathers Hotel its management would be conducted on the highest principles known, and that they would avoid being the tempter of any man. They had to acknow. ledge that the greater portion of mankind would drink alcohol, but there was no need for even a small portion to drink to excess. If no one drank to excess no one would have need be a teetotaler, which was a very trite saying ; but that in Warrington, as everywb r?, people did drink tw excess was only too sadly illustrated from day-to-day. Therefore, recognizing the fact that men did drink immoderately, that a large number drank immoderately, the Corporation of Warrington would be well advised if they either handed over the licence of the Feathers Llotel to the People's Refresh. ment House Association or managed it on the lines of that body. The association was simply a modi- fied copy of tIF? Bishop of Chester's original scheme. His lordship was, in fact, the chairman of the associa- tion, and his scheme was a modified copy of the existing Gothenburg scheme. There was only one principle in it, but it was a principle that entirely altered the at- mosphere of a public house. A tenant was put in charge, but he was not told to sell as much as he could of the brewers' liquor. He was giv-in a fixed salary, whether he sold one barrel a week or 20 or 30; the sales made no difference in his salary.but a diff irence was made so far as the sale of refreshments was concerned. He might sell bread and Ctleebc). a inei.t pa, a bottle ef ginger beer, a glass of lemonade, a cup of coffee or tea, and on every article of that kind he sold he got a commission. Trae whole atmosphere, the whole intention of the public house thus became altered, changed into a place of re- freshment without temptation, as they wished it to be. Now this was not a schema that had not been tried. It was something that people could speak of with experitinal Already the new association—it had not been going i , because ths first annual report had only been recently published—had handed over to the local authorities to , such institutions as the Nursing Associations, sums of £3O, £l5, £lO, and £5, and 5 per cent to the share. lholders of the association ; acd, in addition, from £lOO to £2OO had been handed over to the reserve fund. It ' did not require very much argument to see the vast difference hetweenlan hotel with alman put inlcharge on those principles and an hotel with a brewery at the Clack of it, and a tenant who must of nnessity feel that his brewer would be the more pleased when be sold the most liquor. He (the preaoher) wouhl go so far in bit faith in the schema—because none cf them need he bigots on the tempera ,ca question—as to say that he had had land given to his parish for a large parochial hall and a bowling green by the side of it, and that if anyone present c iuld persuade either a company or a single gentleman to give him £3,000 or £4.000 to corn- plate the scheme at once he would not object to put a licence to it on the principle of the G:thenburg system. So far experience led them to think that that was the only way of curing a great deal of the intemperance of England.

The Chamber of Commerce. A meeting of the council of the Chamber of Com- merce was held at Market (ate Chambers on Mon. • day,tlae new president, Mr C E Parker, presiding. A letter was read from Mr Peter itylands acceptingthe vice-presidency.—A communication was read from the Warrington Trades and La'JOllr Council in refer- ence to the formation of the proposed local Board of Conciliation, and the secretary was requested to write to them suggesting a date for the deputation to meet the Chamber.—The presi• dent,vice-president,secretary and any other member who could make it convenient to attend were , appointed delegates to the annual meeting of tile Associated Chambers, to be held in London en the 12th, 13th,and 14th March.—The programme for the meeting was then considered, and the delegates were instructed to support Vie Manchester tion with reference to the amendment of the patent , laws,and the resolution in favour of the registration of firms, it being understood that the delegates should exercise their discretion with reference to the other resolutions, Some conversation took place with regard to the ter re by which the Cor- poration are arranging to supply electric light to local firms, but the Secretary explained that the matter is at present in its experimental stage and that it would be advisable to defer the discussion until a later date.

SftOUW KB)P A BOTTLI

READY TO RAIW.

Mr. H HAYWARD, of Brown- hills, near Walsall, writes to us That he had the misfortune to fall down while carrying a piece of timber. 35. BRIDGR-1319ERT 1 WARRINGTON. 5. I BRIDGR-STRIIRT

THE friends of roll— Religious Equality. gious equality mustered

in good force- and spirits this week to review the political situation, and they were able to discover many hopeful indica- tions, many signs of coming change. The General Election had not, on the whole, been unkind to them. There were one hundred and fifteen Non- conformists in the House of Commons. Wales had done very well indeed,and if in Scotland the union of two Presbyterian bodies had seemed to give prominence to ecclesiastical over ordinary politics, Captain PIRIE was bold to declare that when the nest movement towards religious equality began the small nations would take their proper place in connection with the advance. The general outlook in England was promising. There was more unity and cohesion amongst the Free Churches, and that alone was a signifi.- ,caut fact. On the other hand, there was more disunion in the Established Church,. and com- peting ideals were influencing the minds of clergy and laity. Some of them desired more freedom, and the Convocations Bill, as Mr CARVELL WILLIAMS regarded it, was an attempt to obtain some, of the results of Disestablishment with- out any of the disadvantages (to Churchmen) of Disendowment. The Ritualistic movement was making for the decline of the; State connection aid, as one of the speakers argued, it must have the effect of educating the laity in the belief that men must pay for the religion in which they be- lieved. The friends of religious equality were not against the clergy of the Establishment but the establishmer t of the clergy, which was quite another matter. Hence there was a certain amount of sympathy with the efforts to obtain freedom though there was none whatever for the attempt to promote Romanism. The Imperial race of England would not again come under the dominion of the POPE. When they looked at Parliament itself,with its present tendencies and majority,there were undoubted apprehensions, and there was evi- dently much work to he doneand many movements to resist. Dr Cuirroan, in a letter he sent apologising for his absence,, however, sounded the true note. It was not to Parliament that the friends of religious equality looked for their principles or their inspiration. It was the sphere of their activity,not the source of their strength. The present Government was reactionary, unfriendly to religious equality, and it had maimed two -asures of importance, the Dissenters Marriage Act and the new 'Burials Act, useful as these meaqures might prove despite their conditions. Tu.) dAes to the clergy were to be renewed. It was much to be feared that there were sinister de- signs on education. Mr EvAx SPICER and Mr CAR- VELL WILLIAMS laid due stress on the dangers im- mediately ahead. The former dwelt on the diffi- culties in the way of training young men and women for the teaching profession without endan- gering their religious rights and independence. The latter feax ed that the new educational policy of the Government was hostile to religious equality and to Nonconformists, and he advised that cer- tain departments of the State should be carefully watched. Captain PIRIR and Mr T TAYLOR in- sisted upon the necessity of combating the mate- rialism of the time, but both were hopeful as to the development of a more aggressive spirit amongst Nonconformists and Liberals generally, and referred in terms of praise to Sir H CAMPBELL.. BANNERMAN, the Liberal Leader. The outlook for religious equality might seeln to be dark, as several speakers observed, but probabiy it was the dark- ness before the dawn. The Free Churches were re- covering their vigour and self-assertion. The general policy of Dig-establishment was making more friends, and new men were coming to the front to take the places of the veterans.

WHATZVEtt may be The Invasion of Cape the ultimate fate of DE Colony. WET, his invasion of Cape Colony cannot be regarded as one of his suc- cesses. He has twice intended to accomplish great things. The first time he never reached the Orange River, and he had to hurry back to his favourite haunts to escape defeat and capture, breaking through the British lines in a desperate charge. His second adventure has been even more

I

disastrous. He has lost his guns and ammunition, and he has not had a day's rest since he entered 'Cape Colony. Pursued, attacked, and defeated, he is once more a fugitive, and his capture, sur- render, or disappearance cannot be averted. His genius for organization and flight has served him Well. Some of his escapades have been wonderful in the extreme. But now that LORD KITCHENER is better supplied with mounted men the Boer guerilla leader cannot hope to repeat his former exploits, even should he be fortunate enough to gather about him fresh bands of fighting men. For the moment, at any rate, we may dismiss him from the mind, ass man who no longer counts as far as Cape Colony is concerned. He is not likely to make any further raids, and it cannot be many Weeks before Cape Colony and its borders are completely cleared of invaders, though the country cannot soon recover from the devastation which has been brought about by the Boers or has been necessary to prevent them from obtaining horses and supplies. The confidence felt at Capetown in the defence of the Colony has been abundantly justified, and our estimate of the dangers to which it was exposed has not been borne out by the facts. The disloyal residents have been gradually lessening. The younger men have been in the War from the first time the enemy crossed the border. Some of them joined the invaders in the early days of the war. Others have since cast in their lot with their comrades of the Orange Colony. A few have been under arms since the Small commandos passed over the drifts and began to spread alarm, preparatory to the expected co- operation of DE War. Older men have remained at home, rendering what assistance they could to the Boer forces in horses, food, and information. 'The great want of the invaders has been ammuni- tion, and this is undoubtedly failing. DE WET'S secret supplies must be getting exhausted, and the I°Yal residents of Cape Colony must be relieved to think that the worst is now over. They have had a terrible time since the war commenced,and if they /relcomed martial law it was because it promised them protection against their neighbours and false friends. We can hardly be surprised that in Botrie parts they have had to maintain a passive To/c. They were surrounded by farmers and towns- people who were in sympathy with the Boers, and they could not do anything that would expose them to risks. In other parts, where they were the majority, they have behaved splendidly, and they deserve the highest praise for their valour and their sacrifices. During the tension oil the last invasion they have assisted the British forces in every way, and the Cape Government is deeply indebted to them for their patriotism. At home We are concerned with their losses, which must have been considerable. Happily, they can now look forward to a quieter state of thing. As soon as the remnants of the invading bands have been cleared out the Colony will be safe from future attack. Concerted measure of defence will be made in the light of a bitter experience, and the Orange tOers will see the wisdom of remaining at horn, if they have not the grace to submit to a superior force.
