MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

With the advent of March comes the very near approach of the triennial elections to the County Council under the Local Government Act of 1888, and a few days later there will be the triennial elections of poor law Guardians under the Act of 1894. With regard to the comparatively new County Councils throughout the provinces, it may be said that the work accomplished hitherto in county government has for the most part been carried on efficiently and with due regard to the pockets of the ratepayers. The greater portion of ' the business, performed as it is in committee, and: formally endorsed as a rule with no great amount of discussion at the public meetings of the Councils does not come vrry prominently under the public notice. But it is none the less valuable and import- ant on that account. Considering, as has just been suggested, that the work and machinery of county government is as yet really new, it may be said that the wheels run with rerwirkable smoothness, and that the Councils have fully justified their being called into existence. Toe fact that they have pro- ceeded thus far with so little friction accounts for the compara. ively small number of contestei elections, and augurs well, perhaps, for the future.

It was thought by some people in 1894, before the Local Government Act of that year came into operation, that when the new Boards of Guardians were created by purely popular election, there would be a less rigid hold on the public purse strings, and that the principles of a stern political economy would give place to the promptings of a sympathy nearly akin to sentimentality. It was believed in particular that a democratically elected Board of Guardians would be much more disposed to grant out-relief and be less exacting in the mat- ter of the Workhouse test. Figures relating to six years operations under the Act, however, clearly demonstrate how ill-founded such a belief was. Yet the Act of 1894 brought about considerable changes in the method of election, of which many persons still require to be reminded. There is now no elction, for instance, in a rural parish, as the rural district Councillors act as guardians for their parishes. Ex officio guardians were abolished One- third of the whole Board of Guardians retire on April 15th in each year, though the County Council may direct that all the guardians shall retire to- gether every third year. A chairman and vice- chairman, and not more than two other persons, may be elected by the guardians from outside their own body. The meetings and proceedings of a board of guardians have been assimilatea to those of a District Council, and are now regulated by the provisions of the Public Health Act, 1875, with respect to the meetings and proceedings of a local board.

The Executive Committee of the Association of Poor Law Unions appear to have reasonable ground of complaint against the Local Government, for having decided that the new dietary order shall come into force on the 25th inst , without paying any attention to the views expressed at their res- pective meetings, by two hundred Unions scattered all over the country, replies from which had been received by the Association, and had led the latter to draw up a list of recommendations, intended for the Board's consideration. The opinions of all these Guardians thus count for nothing, and the order in regard to the new dietary scales must be carried out in spite of them

Poor-law returns show that in England and Wales during the last quarter o f 1900 the number of paupers in every thousand of the population was 21.0 at the end of October,2l•4 at the end of Novem- ber, and 21.6 at the end of Dec3mber. Comparison of the figures with those for previous years indicate that, as regards each of the three months of the quarter, the proportion was smaller last year than in any of the forty three years preceding. This is very satisfactory as far as it goes; but for a great number of years we have had the anomaly of a decreasing rate of pauperism, with an increasing rate of expenditure upon it,

From various parts of the provinces, particularly in the larger town, come reports of the noble response made to the latest call to arms. Only a month has elapsed since the call was issued, and during those four weeks eleven thousand have Joined the Yeomanry. Indeed, the spontaneity that has. marked the response to the call has proved almost embarrassing to the authorities, who did not expect the recruiting to proceed so rapidly. The consequence has been to show a little unprepared- ness at Aldershot and elsewhere. Now that the full significance of the movement is realised, however, the Government appears to be making every effort to provide proper accsmmodation for the men, and to furnish the necessary equipment. Already drafts of the Yeomanry have embarked for South Africa. Seven hundred left Aldershot on Monday for South- ampton, and more than a thousand from Woolwich left for Capetown on Tuesday. Even if the Yeo- manry are not required for actual fighting the men may always find congenial work as mounted police under Baden-Powell.

Municipalisation grows apace, whatever some of the higher authorities may advance against it. Here Is Ashton-under-Lyne Town Council deciding to purchase apparatus for sterilising " humanis- ing" milk, and determining to open a shop at which it is to be sold. This, we are told, is being done with the object of reducing the high infantile mortality, which is said to be due to improper feed- ing. It is proposed, however, to sell the sterilised milk not only in bottles for infants' food, but also in pint and quart measures for general purposes. The Corporation are said to anticipate a considerable basil:less in this direction, and to believe that eventually it will become a source of profit- But we have yet to learn What Mrs Grundy, in the form of the Local Government Board, or of the opponents of these municipalising tendencies, have to say. The milk sterilising innovation is about the most startling one has hitherto heard of. But possibly there may be something equal to it yet to come, and one ought not to be surprised at anything.

----- School Boards in various districts are agreeing to petition the House of Commons respecting the de- cision in the High Court concerning the teaching of <inience and art in their schools. It is impossible to suppose that the Government will allow any retrograde movement to affect the cause of popular education;but none the less it seems the duty of every School Board to press the matter upon the attention of Parliament. It may be in- ferred from the Duke of Devonshire's statement in the House of Lords, however, which has so astonished Earl Spencer, Lord Battersria and other ardent educationists, that nothing will be done in either House while the questions raised by the judgment in "Regina v. Cockerton " remain un- settled. The Duke considers it will be highly inex- pedient "either to encourage or permit " School Boards to make further advances in providing education of a type which, he says, was " certainly not contemplated by Parliament when School , Boards were established."

EMPLOYERS.

An unique discovery in labour circles was made the other week, when it became generally known that men engaged 'alongside ship," as it is called, were in receipt of no extra pay for overtime; and as the matter affected the Upper Mersey wat.ermen and Porters very materially it was at once taken in hand by the Executive Committee of that body and the employers approached on the subject. A satis- factory issue has been arrived at, and the Execu- tive on Monday afternoon, met at their head- quarters, High-street, Runcorn, and decided to accept the offar of the combined employers of 5i per hour per man. Overtime to count from eight p.m., to seven a.m., and from 2 p.m. on Saturdays. Pay to commence from the time of receiving a definite order to stand by for work alongside ship. There has been no dispute, and no coercion, and the very amicable settlement of a practical new issue s a matter for all round congratulation. WEST BANE MISSION, WIDNES.

In connection with the above mission there was a very good attendance on Saturday evening at the 1 usual weekly concert. " Killarney " and "The Rose of Allendale" were capitally given by Miss Wilson, while Mr J Bingham scored a distinct triumph with "The Pardon Came Too Late" and "See that my grave's kept green." Mr II Dwerryhouse con- tributed a violin solo with no little taste; while Mr T Richards, for a selection on the fairy bells, was warmly applauded The recitations, "I cannot bear it now,"" Which One?" and the "The Little House- keeper," by Misses Annie and Alice Bingham, proved that their oratorical ability was of no mean order. The duet "If we try," by Miss and Mr Tickle, was sang with much expression, while the former further enhanced her reputation by the rendering of "Her Boy, Jack." The pianoforte solos, "The Referee from Klondyke," and "The Grecian March." by Miss Leach, was much enjoyed. An Old Woman's Shockinc, Death. An inquest was conducted at thet'Police Station, by Mr S Brighouse, County !Coroner, on Tuesday afternoon, relative to the death of Hannah Maria Miller, 73 years of age, who resided with her hus- band at 6, Hart's-place, off Bridge-street, and was burnt to death on Saturday evening. —Elizabeth ' Seddon, 3, Hart's-place, daughter of tha deceased, gave evidence to the effect that she last saw her mother alive at 5.30 on Saturday evening. She then appeared very feeble 89 usual. About 6.40 the same evening her (witness's) nephew informed her that smoke was issuing from his grandmother's house. She at once proceeded there and found the kitchen full of smoke and in darkness. She ran upstairs to look for her mother but could not find her. She also went into the yard but de- ceased was not there. She then returned to the kitchen and her foot caught against something. She stooped down, and found her mother lying on the floor with her clothing smouldering. Dr Geary was sent for and found the deceased was terribly burnt about the body and face. Life was extinct. Deceased was in the habit of lighting the gas, which projected out over the lire-place, with a long piece of paper and witness thought she must have been in the act of lighting it, when by some means her clothing became ignited. Deceased's husband who was 78 years of age, left the house at six!o'clock,and consequently the deceased was alone, A verdict of "Accidental death " was returned.

..IRIO.US FIRE AT WIDNRS

At one o'clock on Monday morning a serious fire broke out in a dwelling-house at 3, Luton-street, occupied by William Hatton, hi n wife, family, and a lodger named Enoch Jones. About eleven o'clock on the Sunday, Mr and Mrs Hatton retired to bed, Jones retiring half an hour later, leaving the house all right. At one o'clock he was awakened by the bedroom being full of smoke. He at once raised an alarm and together with Jones went downstairs and found the lower rooms also full of smoke, which was issuing from a pantry underneath the staircase As it was apparently impossible to extinguish the flames a messenger was despatched to the Police Station and very promptly P.C's Evans, Davidson, and Messenger arrived on the scene of the confla- gration. The neighbours were also aroused, and very quickly buckets of water were employed in extinguishing the flames.lnformation was also con- veyed to the Fire Brigade Station, and but little time was lost before eight firemen under foreman Beesley arrived, bringing witu them a band-reel. There was a good pressure of water, and as they commenced to play on the flames the tire visibly de- creased and it was soon extinguished. At the time the fire brigade commenced operations Mrs Hatton was in bed with the two children. She managed to get downstairs with one, but was forced to leave the other child in bed, owing to the staircase being on fire and the large volume of smoke, which was emitted. Jones made a gallant attempt at rescue, but was beaten back, and it seemed as though the child would be lost. P.C. Davidson however, made an attempt which ended more fortunately, piercing the fire and smoke and bringing the child, aged about two years,out of harm's way. The damage is considerable,and how the fire originally commenced has not yet been definitely ascertained.

THE LATE DR RICHARDS, WIDNES. On 'A ednesday afternoon the mortal remains of the late Councillor Dr Richards, of Widnes, who died on Monday, were laid to rest in St Mary's Churchyard. At three o'clock the members of the Town Council assembled and drove to the house of the deceased in Mersey-road. Subsequently a force of police under Sub-Inspector Strickland arrived, and forming into procession, the party marched to the church close by. An impressive service was held. The Vicar (Rev C B Stocks) eulogised in high terms the character of the deceased. A SOLDIER AND HIS RAILWAY TRAVELLING, Peter Cropper, a soldier of the 18th Hussars. pleaded guilty at Frodsham Petty Sessions on Wed- nesday to travelling on the railway from Runcorn to Helsby on the previous night without a ticket. A railway inspector proved the charge. The prisoner admitted that he was absent without leave and said be was on his way to Chester Castle to give himself up. If the magistrates would discharge him he would go back to his regiment like a man. He was fined 53 or seven days' imprisonment. A THIEF WELL PUNISHED. The Runcorn magistrates on Wednesday morning sentenced Sarah Helsby, a well-known character with five convictions for larceny standing to her credit for the last year, to three months hard labour for stealing 23 61 out of a purse.—lt ap- peared prisoner went to the house of Mrs George Walton in Ellesmere-street, and asked for per- mission to wash. This Mrs Walton kindly granted, and allowed her to stay until about a quarter to twelve, when Ilelsby went out and returned about half-past. In the morning Helsby had seen Mrs Walton take some money out of a purse which she took and placed in"a cupboard. On the occasion of Helsby's return Mr; Walton was obliged to go to the.: Stores cart," outside her house, and whilst absent Helsby took the half-crown from the purse. —Prisoner admitted the offence to P.C.'s Shaw and Carrington who arrested her. SHOCKING NEWIRCT OF CH/LDRAN AT WIDNES. On Thursday, at Widnes Petty Sessions. Peter and Ellen Platt, 6, Dowell-street, Widnes, were charged with neglecting their three children on February 25 and other dates. The evidence showed that a National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children official found the children in a dirty condition and ill-clad. The house was in a shock- ing state. Both vrere sent to gaol for three months —John .Reynolds was, similarly charged with neglecting five children on February 21 and ether dates —After hearing 011ie evidence the Bench adjourned the case for a month.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORIVESPONDBITY.I The County Council elections for the locality have just worked out as arranged ; save, of course, that some irresponsible persons should have nomina- ted Mr R H Posnett in the Liberal interest, without first consulting him. He, however, at once with- drew; and the retiring Councillors, Messrs A R Norman and E Imison, resume their offices, having our undivided confidence.

The Runcorn Board of Guardians is a body up-m which these has always been experienced a difficulty in getting anyone to sit as representing the town of Runcorn. The Rev Dr Gastaldi, Mr John Meadowcroft, and Mr Robert Wright are understood not to be seeking re-election : and so far no names are forthcoming of persons likely to fill the vacan- cies. Messrs s Taylor and I R Norman, T Lea and T Reynolds, have for some time been the only work- ing reliresentatives of the district; and,should they wish it, will be returned unopposed in their several wards. The reason for the difficulty in getting representative men at Dutton, is apparently, their difficulty in creating " a f allowing " ; and those who accept the office find themselves called upon to discharge duties of such a routine nature, in such an un-get-e-able place, that very soon they con- sider" the game is not worth the candle."

_ --- Indeed, it may be said with truth, that the only guardian representing Runcorn, in a position to do more than already indicated is Mr A R Norman (the genial chairman of the House Committee), and the work he does in his department is well known and appreciated. But, notwithstanding all the peculiar circumstances which go to make a Guardian's posi- tion almost an honorary one, at the next election we will be face to face with three vacancies, and should be on the look out for gentlemen willing to accept office on our behalf as Guardians of the Poor, and failing gentlemen, ladies' services right be secured.

---- DO,Steele, whose death took place at Frodshaii on Friday lest, was practically the first gentleman to put forward the qaistion of "A Cottage Hospital for Runcorn," though at the time he dia so the proposal was very coldly received.

FREE CHURCHES UNITED MISSION. The Closing Meetings.

Tuesday evening witnessed the closing meeting in connection with the Free Churches United Mission in Warrington. From every point of view, the mission has been a great success, and the Free Churches have been so encouraged by the results as to decide to continue a Sunday evening United Mission service during March. The first of these services will be held to-morrow (Sun- day) evening in the Winwick-street Co-operative , Hall. At the farewell meeting on Tuesday, suitable expression was given of the indebted- ness of the Free Churches of Warrington to the Rev. John Gould for his excellent work ' during the mission. The hope has been freely expressed that if at any future time it is decided to hold a united mission, such as that just closed, a return visit of the Rev. J. Gould would go far towards securing its success. Mr. Gould's sympa- thetic and soul-stirring addresses have commended themselves to all who have had the privilege of listening to him, and the large audiences at the ser- vices have been evidence of his popularity.

Saturday night's Temperance Meeting.

One of the most successful temperance meetings which have been held in Warrington was that arranged for in connection with the Mission and held in Bold- street Wee'cyan Chapel on Saturday evening. The attendance was large ; the addresses were excellent . and the number of persons who signed the pledge at the close was most encouraging. Mr R A Naylor presided, and there were also present on the platform. the Mayor- eel (Mrs H Roberts), Mrs R Garnett, Miss Naylor, and Miss Bessie Dutton, the Revs J Yonge, E T Reed, T Bishop, C H Hocken, J F Matthews. J I. Milne, and E G King (of Liverpool). It was announced that the RIV J Gould (the missioner), who had suffered from a cold during toe week, was taking a well-merited rest, and would not be present, though he was in full sympathy with the temperance movement, While the people were assembling the United Free Church choir sang several hymns. Mr W Brouth c )nducl.d, and Mr F Hickman presided at the organ. The mee:ing was opened with singing and prayer. The Camesuar observed that it wag a pleasure to him to preside over such a respectable gathering, lie was afraid Ulf 3 were not many drunkards present, so he did not suppose he needed to urge the plea addressed to the drunkard that it would be a good thing for himself and those connected with him if he would sign the pledge. He did not know, however, whether it had struck them that there was an much necessity for the respectable part of a community to sign the pledge as there was for the drunkard. Of course the drunkard was a degraded being. He robbed himself of the reason which Gd had given him, he dethroned his manhood. He was are obj •ct of pity, but many of the poor creatures they saw brought before the magistrates for this offence might, had they been brought up under better conditions, have lived far different lives, and they in another station could not be sufficiently thankful to their parents for teaching them the great principles of total abstinence. (Hear, hear.) There Was one thiog cartain,it was utterly impossible for those who practised those principles to become drunkards. Befoie a man became a drunkard he had to become a drinker, and the high road to drunkenness was along the way of moderation. (Hear, hear ) If they never became moderate drinkers they would never become drunkards. In the blunt phrase- ology of one of the early temperance men, "if you never take the first glass the devil himself cannot make you have a second." (Hear, hear.) Many people to whom drink was no temptation considered there was no necessity for them to sign the pledge, but looking at the question in the light of the Christian religion it was the duty of Christian people to become pledged total abstainers so that they might have a greater influence with those who had fallen. Though there was not a single drunkard present there might be Christian men and women who did not consider it obligatory to sign the pledge for the sake of others. He would ask them, however, for the sake of poor sugaring humanity to take this question into their most serious consideration, and say whether it was not their duty—seeing that the country was cursed so grievously by strong drink, know- ing that because of it thousands of poor little children were badly-clad and ill-fed and scarcely knew what it was to have the love and affection of a kind father and mother, seeing also that our prisons, workhouses and lunatic asylums were mainly populated because of the same evil—as Chrietian people, and for the sake of the Master who made such sacrifice for them, to make this little aacrifiee for Him. (Applause). The Rev E G KING, of Liverpool, delivered an im• preesive address in which he was able from his own experiences, to present a vivi pietnee of the evils attend- ant upon intemperance. Ho eloquently appealed to all who had not already done so to sign the pledge. He said he assumed that there Wtr3 some present who did not censider it a eecred duty to sign the pledge. To them he would lay down the proposition that it was the bounden duty of every Christian man and woman t 3 be a total abstainer. If they could get every Christian man and every Christian woman to sign the pledge, they could bring the stronees: Government that ever existed to its knees within twelve months. (Hear, hear). They would then be within measurable distance of a final solu- tion of the great drink ispaestisn, and their country would anon be purged of the greatest curse that had ever afflicted a nation in ancient or modern times. Be might be a bigot or a fanatic, but for the life of him he could not understand how any man with an en- lightened Christian conscience could have any other than one opinion about the drink traffic. A gentlemen told the audience at one of his (the speaker's) Band of Hope meetings, that so many hundred ministers of religion were shareholders in the great brewery c mcerns of the country. He (the speaker) did not believe for a single moment, and he told the gentle- man, that any of those ministers were ministers of the Nonconformist Churches. (Hear, hear.) There were two reasons. In the first place they had not the money to invest—(laughter)—and secondly they had far too much conscience to invest money if they had it in such a nefarious trade. A man who would be a minister of Christ's holy religion and draw part of his income from the ruin of the bodies and souls of the people was a monster wherever he might be, and if there was no other church on the face of God's earth he (the speaker) would never darken the doors of his sanctuary to listen to the Gospel from his lips, (Applause). Ile believed the time was coming when there would be such a strong Christian sentiment in all their churches that it would be utterly impossible for a minister, or an offise bearer, deacon, steward, local preacher, Sunday mehool teacher, Christian Endeavourer or any man to hold any office who was not clear on this matter. (Hear, hear.) Not only upon scientific, economic and physical grounds was it desirable that all should be total abstainers but on the highest moral and spiritual grounds. The man who teuched drink was always in danger. There was death in it. When it took hold of • Man it Was almost impossible for him to give it up. (Applause.) Mr. Lim Ricaannsou very effectively sang "Dire to be a Daniel," the choir joining in the chorus. The Rev C FINCH also delivered a telling address. H. related some of his experiences in temperance work, and told the audiences why various people he had known had signed the pledge. In conclusion he made an earnest appeal to those whe hai not done so already, to sign the pledge. A number of pledge books were taken round by mem- bers of the British Women's Temperance Association and a number of signatures were obtained. The services of Mr and Mies Naylor were fittingly acknowledged on the proposition of the Rev. JOHN YONGE, seconded by Mr. Councillor Jestiss.EVANS. _ .

Mr NAYLOR in acknowledging the compliment, said he thought that the vote of thanks should have been given to the speaker. Ile hid been very pleased to see so many people join the cause. He announced that the number of pledges signed was 43 of men and 33 of women. Oners signed the pledge afterwards. After the singing of a hymn the meeting concluded with prayer by the Rev John Yonge.

The Services on Sunday.

The attendanc.s at the two services on Sunday were larger than ever, and some were unable to obtain admis- sion at the evening meeting. The service in the after- noon was for men only, and a cordial invitation was given to members of Pleasant Sunday Afternoon Socie- ties to attend. The Wycliffe, Buckley-street, and Young Men's Pleasant Sunday Afternoon Societies were present, having marched in procession from their various centres to the Bold-street Wesleyan Church. Over 1,000 men were present, and the interior of the building presented an imposing and encouraging appearance. The His- sioner was accompanied on the platform by the local Free Church ministers and the secretaries. "The meeting was opened by the singing of a hymn and then prayer was conducted by the Rev John Yonge, the lesson being read by the Rev H G Roberts, M A. The address was given by the Missioner, who based his remarks on the words "All things are now ready." He urged upon those present the necessity of surrendering themselves to God while they were young. They were all, he said, born in the arms of Jesus, but by-and-bV , they fell sway. Some of them would, no doubt say that they would leave off conversion to a future period, but the present was the best time. The meeting was closed by prayer. There was a number of inquirers at the close. The evening meeting, held after the ordinary services in the churches was largely attended, the vast building being crowded. It was expected that an overflow meeting would have been held in the schoolroom, but no arrangements had been made for it, and as a conse- quence many had to be refused admission to the Church As the vast congregation joined in the praise of God,the sight was a deeply impressive one. In the words of the Rev J Yonge, " A more imposing sight has never been seen In Warrington." The Rev John Gould was on the rostrum, being supported by the Revs J Yonge, H G Roberts, E T Reed, J T Schofield, J F Matthews, and Masan Shaw Green, 3 Evans and Twigs. An earnest

and powerful address was delivered by the Missioner, and the in:ieting field afterward.' was well attended. Mr Levi Richardson sing in his usually efficient manner, " Come unto me ye weary,"

The Campaign at Runcorn.

. _ The simultaneous Mission, so far as Runcorn was concerned, came to a conclusion on Tuesday evening,and has been productive of much goo I. Oa Sunday the Free Church ministers exchanged pulpits, and a !mania service for men was held in Bethesda Cougregatioaal Church, which was addressed by the Rev A Wells. On Sunday night there was an united " after-meeting" at St Paul's Church. The congregations of all the Free Churches in the town, forming in procession, headed by their respective choirs, marched to that place of worship for the purpose, and took part in a never-to-be-forgotten gathering, in which the;wbule of the ministers of the Free Churches officiated. The music was especially taking, the united choirs singing the verses, and the whole congregation joining in the refrain. The noon-day services, bible-readings, and evening services held throughout the week have also been very encouraging.

SIR JOHN BRUNNER ON LAND TAXATION.

On Wednesday evening the Wavertree Liberal Also. ciatioa and the Wavertree Women's Liberal Association held a social evening in the Town Hall, Wavertree. Sir J T Brunner, Bart., M.P., presided, and there was a numerous attendance of ladies and gentlemen, The Chairman, in the course of an introductory address, devoted himself largely to the subject of taxa- tion. There was a considerable number of landowners in this country, though they were only a handful as compared with the total population, upon whom is was very anxious to put a fair—only a fair— amount of taxation. (Applause.) There were ,no people in all the world, those of the United Statile perhaps excepted, who had gained so enormouily by the progress made during the period of the late QtleaeS reign than the landowners of Great Britain. (gear, hear.) A: the earns time there was no set of people who had done less to deserve their share of that pros- perity. (Applause.) He remembered nearly a score of years ago shocking a nuMber of Cneshire people,selocan ha respected, by centering that the mere receiver or rent was the most useless of human beings. (Hear,lhesse, and applause.) High rents were very good for those who received them, but high rents did not increase the prosperity of those who pad them, and in this blessed country three wto- paid the rents, paid the taxes, and those who received the rents almost escipol taxatioa. 01 that point ho he lieved he was at one with every individual on that plat' form, and, he hoped in the Hall, in believing that real estate should pay its fair tArt.. of taxation. (Applause.) There was closeto where they where assembrel a plot off land, which he had known since he was a lad. it that time be believed it would not have fetched £6O to an acre, whereas now he very much doubted if the owner would take £1 600 to £1,700 an acre for it.. What had caused the difference in value bat the thought, aadi industry, and expenditure of the people of Liverpool'. (Hear, hear and applauqe.) Wnere was the justim Of law which decreed tn.*: sa woo kept his lind! out of the market should have all that benefit, out of Cho thought and industry of the people.without contributing anything towards public burdens ? People to whom be spoke about the necessity for reform in this were totaky in accord with him if they did not know that he IMO 11. Radical. (Laughter) Speaking of public service be wanted all English people to honour it, but refrain fro= paying undue deference to mere wealth. The influences of wealth and the power of money were growing in this country in a fashion that boded dreadful ills for time future. Society in London had been debauched hr gambling, especially gambling in gold-mining shares, Ws such an extent that the honesty of their legislattue and public life was in danger.

THE 20TH CENTURY.

Do you know that it has often been said that great events happen at the end and at the beginning of a Century ? No doubt we shall have the usual prophets forecasting the probabilities of all sorts of startling occurrences to mark the conamencement of the 20th Century, but there is no one bold enough to predict that there will be less of pain and suffering, less of human misery and disease in the World. The lot of human kind is to suffer, and the greatest benefactor is he who does something to mitigate pain and affliction. "Never prophesy till you know "is a wise saying, and we adept this maxim when we say that Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters is destined to occupy a greatly extended sphere of usefulness in the new Century. It has been proved by thousands of sufferers to be the one safe and certain remedy for all Nervous Disorders, Low Spirits, Depres Mon, Liver Complaints, Indigestion, and Genera Weakness.

BURGLAR-PROOF_ AND FIRE-PROOF

GLASS. Interesting Experiment at St. Helens.

At Me=srs Pi—lkington's Glassworks, St. Helens, on Tuesday in the presence of a number of well-known engineers and representatives of fire insurance com- panies, a most interesting demonstration was given of the qualities of the patent wire glass which the firm have been making for two or three years. Messrs Pilkine- ton ate the only makers of this description of glass, which is largely used for skylights and other places where there is a danger of is tailing on to passers-by or people using premises which are lighted in this manner. the glass is made by a special process, in which wire netting is rolled in the centre of the plate. This makes the glass burglar-proof, but the firm de- sired to demonstrate its utility chiefly in the direction of preventing the spread of fire, and also in enabling an outbreak of fire to be very easily discovered. Glass win- dows are always Letter in this respect than iron shut- ters, but the glass windows have always carried the dis- advantage of being easily broken. A special building had been erected in the works, fitted with windows of wire glass, and one window fitted with ordinary thiek olateglass. By means of gas jets the inside was heated 2,500 degrees Fain. The plateglass window quickly broke, but the wire glass, although almost melted by the excessive heat, splintered by the jets of water which were played on to it, remained in position, and after these most severe tests the windows were practically in- tact. The demonstration was carried out under the supervision of Mr Lee Pilkington, and was exeeedingly successful. The glass windows were set in wrought. iron frames, which have been found to be most suitable and least affected by the extreme heat.

Cheshire Lines Ambulance Work.

Keen interest was again manifested in the annual ambulance competition in conne6tion with the Cheshire Lines Railway. As in previous years, the event took place in a waiting-room at the Central Station, Liverpool, on Friday. Nine squads from Liverpool. Manchester. and Warrington,entered. Dr F de B Pim, of Barrowford, was the judge, and the testa through which each team went were smartly performed,and afforded a fine exhibition of the skill railway servants have acquired in the important art of aiding the injured. At the close Mr David Meld- rum (manager Cheshire Lines) distributed the prizes to the winners. Among the company present were ' Messrs J 0 Ellison, St, John Ambulance Associa- tion; George Mason, Glegge Thomas, S Bening (station master), Cantata Waring (Latchford), Dr Battle ( Warrington Infirmary), JMedley, J Bushell, T Pratt (engineers' department), Alfred Manners (Huskisson), R Powell (Brunswick), W Chawner (Midland Railway), Langley Thompson, 11 T Heath- cote (Birkenhead), A Hay, J Buckley, R Heaton (Warrington), P Bamford, J Leather, E P Connolly, W Carson, A E Edwards, &c.—Mr Meldrum , announced the winning teams as follows :—Glaze- , brook and Cadishead Station, first: Warrington Central Station, second ; Warrington Engineers, third. The first prize consists of an elaborate and costly silver cup, which the winners hold till other rivals wrest it from them. Each squad consisted of five men, and besides the cup each man in the win- ning squad received a handsome travelling bag. The two other squads also carried off a silver cup each. Each of those who in the test won the second prize further received a case of carvers, and the members of the third ,corps each got an umbrella. —The Chairman called attention to the fact that men belonging to their ambulance corps had volunteered to go to the I front. Several were accepted by the War Office. Some were still in South Africa and three War- rington men—Williams, Hughes, and Holloway— who had been in the Transvaal, had returned and were present in the room. (Applause). Mr Mel- drum said that Mrs Meldrum had deeply regretted her inability to be present to give out the prizes. He then alluded to the services of Mr E 0 Lloyd, as hon. secretary to the ambulance classes. (Applause). Mr Lloyd had put his heart and soul into the under- taking, and it was due to him that such excellent arrangements were always made at the cm:T-0- tlOll3. (,Applause).—Acordiil vote of thanks was conveyed to Mr Lloyd, who suitably resnomied, and a .similar compliment was paid to Mr Meldrum his services in the chair.—rhe winning team will represent—:according to the custom each year—the Cheshire Lines in the National Railway Competi- tion at the Crystal Palace.

NORTHERN UNION. LANCASHIFiE SENIOR COMPETITION.

Warrington v. Stockport.

The " coup de grace "administered by Warrington to Stockport in their return engagement on Saturday placed the Cheshire men in the uoenviable posi- tion of being the most probable candidates for the wooden spoon. By the defeat the " Wire- pullers " also avenged the result of the first meeting when, it will be remembered, the " Joy- ful News " brigade so unexpectedly defeated the ' West Lancashire club. Playing with a stiff breeze, the Warringtouians were the first aggressors, but so strong did the visitors' vanguard retaliate that the efforts of Bate, Isherwood, and Harris were chequered. Strong forward play by Oram was instrumental in forcing play at half-way, and Robinson, ever alert, took advantage of some slow exchanges by the home backs and caused many anxious ailments to the home sup- pn-ters. Hallam cleaed splendidly. and then exchange kicking went in favour 4 f Isherwood and Harris, the forwards supplementing their effrirts by forcing Simister to adopt a stric-ly d ifenstve attitude. A. rare opening made by Duckworth saw Harris utilise his great strength wathout result, and for his pains the Warrirgtonian was da:naged. However, the attack was maintained, and after Bate had slipped when scor- ing was within his grasp, the ball was whipped out by Duckworth to Bate, who promptly handed over to Isherwood, and the latter serving Fish, that slippery mortal worked himself into position and dropped a fine goal. For a time the Warringtonians appeared to rest on their laurels, and Stockport, taking advantage of the state of lethargy, made matters rather warm for Hallam and Co., but failed in their laudable efforts to sore. At the interval Warrington were leading by two goals and a try (seven points) to nil. Morrison having adored a try, and Fish kicked a goal. Resuming, the Warringtonians made the pace a cracker, and Isherwood placed the ball so judiciously that Fish defeated Simister for possession, and it was a case of hare and bounds, with big odds on-the former. Fish's try was a beauty, and his judgment in fielding the ball Was splendid. Stockport were by no mems daunted at this last success of the Warringtonians, and their tackling of the aggressive locals was charac tensed by robustness and accuracy, which stood out as conspicuous features, in their play. Hocken- hall, the right mere of the home team, was so badly hurt in one tackle that he was carried off the field, taking no further part in the match Eventually the Cheshire boys bad a look in, and Worsley, takiag up an opening, paved the way for Robinson to score SGock- port's solitary point. Near the close the game got very warm,and from a vigorous charge Morrison was ordereo off, much to that player's diicomforture. The end arrived with the visitors soundly trounced by two goals two tries (ten pcints) to one try (three points).

===== 0002601_19010301_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
Runcorn Defeated at Oldham.

It was with rather a feeling of misgiving that Oldham entertained Runcorn on Saturday at Watersheddings, the Cheshire fifteen being recognised as an awkward lot to contend with. Up to the meeting on Saturday the Mersey eiders had the better of the exchanges this season, the match at Runcorn, when the Oldham team went down by three goals to one, creating a feeling of uncertainty as to the issue on Saturday. It was also largely due to this factor, no doubt, that a crowd of something like 12,000 people churned up the sludge Watersheddings way to see the exhibition. Hezzsy and T D Davies were absent from the Oldham third line, and in their stead Civil and Fletcher manipulated the leather. Moffatt was Oldham's absentee forward, and Ellis took his place. Strange to say the Runcorn third line was not intact, Inglesfield making his debut vice Butterworth, and B Jones actiag wing three-quarter in lieu of D Jones. The ground was rather heavy ; des- pite the appearance of consistency on the surface it was ploughed to a considerable depth* From its covering of straw the surface of the ground had a tinge of chaff, and in that respect was the entire opposite of the game about which there was no chaff whatever. It was fought out in real earnest, and Runcorn must admit that they were defeatel at thPir own game. Their remarkable following up b iffies the coolest defence, and when it suits their book to pin the play to a particular quarter—near their oppon- ents' goal line for preference their modus operandi is difficult to combat. Of this Oldham haled recently had a sample and retnem wring their Broughton Rangers experience, they did not leave the game entirely to the handling eystem. They footed it merrily,and settled on the leather excellently, so well, in fact, as to have the better of the first half. The game was much on the Oldham left, and determined attack meeting stubborn defence,loelesfield and Houghton were kept exceedingly busy. Williams had been dulled down near the Runcorn quarter line, but danger had not been averred, for the same player, receiving a capital pass originating from near the scrucnna age, romped over at the corner. No goal was kicked. The next try, while play was in the earlier half, went to Civil's credit, the ex - Northamptonian striding vigorously in the direction of the goal-line and, getting near, and falling, he planted the ball across the Rahicon amid cheers. Ferguson kicked a capital anal, which the 101dhainers hailed with delight as putting the issue beyond doubt. In a scramble near midfield, Messenger mistook the back of Arthur Lees's head for a punching ball, and Frater having some misunderstanding, the referee exerted his authority, and it b 'came a case of exeunt Fester and Messenger. The packs were thus reduced to seven aside. It was patent to the on- lookers that there was a good deal of feeling introduced. Thus the game progressed spiritedly. The nearest approach to scoring that Runcorn had was when Ingle's. field, after a haphazard rush, hal picked up and crossed the line. Just at the psychological moment he lost the ball, which Civil promptly fell upon. The game at the interval was eight points in Oldham's favour.. . .

The second Calf was hotly fought. Runcorn's admitted I capacity to stay being again proved. Either they were weak on the line, or the Oldham defence was in a par- ticularly invulnerable mood, but scoring did not take place. The second half was about twelve minutes advanCed when Inglesfield scored rather easily, the Old- ham three-quarters scarcely seeming to know who had the ball. Jolley kicked badly, and play settled down near the centre. On the right wing Oldham 'did some , attacking, but not of a character calculated to score, and Elou ?hton, for roughly attending . Fletcher, was loudly hooted from the stand. Another incident was the seizure of a Runcorn forward by a touch-judge, and the referee apps tred in the double capacity of judge and adviser. Fletcher'a try in the second half, IfOCTI which, however, no goal was kicked, put the Issue well beyond the realms of doubt and with pure defence on Oldham's part play came to an end with Runcorn in the rearward position. Last year Runcorn took four points out of Oldham, and out of five consecutive matches with Runcorn this is the first match in which Oldham have proved victorious. The Oldham forward exhibition gave satisfaction, and at half-back, while Lawton played a good game, Arthur Lees was particu- larly clever, especially in defending when Runcorn hovered too near the line. Thomas and Houghton both played capitally at full-back, the Runcorn man scarcely being so strong on the defence as we have seen him on previous occasions. Civil pleased the Oldham folks,and his try was the result of a rattling run. Jolley at half- back played a fine game round the "serum," but such was the close work of the Oldham vanguard that Jolley's quest for the ball just behind his comrades' pedal er- re males was often in vain.

Warrington and District Milk Dealers' Association.

In connection with the above association a sup- per was held the other evening at the Wimmarleigh Cate Over 50 members accepted the invitation of the President (Mr Jacob Sankey), and the proceed- ings passed off very satisfactorily. After supper the President proposed "Success to the Association," and referred at length to important matters con- nected with the trade, and the progress that had been made in the work of the association since it had been formed.—Mr Benjamin Grimshaw proposed "The health of Mr and Mrs Sankey: and his remarks were supplemented by Mr G Mather (Fearnbead), both referring in high terms to their career and success in life. The toast was drunk with musical honours. The health of tke Vice- President (Mr Robinson), the Treasurer (Mr War- burton), and the Secretary (Mr Ford) were also submitted and duly responded to, A musical enter- tainment was given by Mr J M Greenhalgh, in which the Misses Tunstall, Sutton, and Jones, and Mr GrJunds (humorist) took part with their usual ability. The singing of the National Anthem brought a very enjoyable evening to a close.

ANALYSIS OF WATERS.

Mr Reginald Potts, clerk to the County Council, wrote as follows i—The County Medical Officer of flealta suggested to the Public Health Committee of this Council at its last meeting that your Rural District Council should be aßked to replace 5 local supplies, sampled as Nos. 514, 518, 519, 520, and 523 in the report of the County Anilyst for the year ending 30. h September last, by town'swater. I shall be glad if you will bring this suggestion under the notice of your District Council in due course, and intimate to me for the information of my committae, the action if any they take thereon. Mr. Farrington reported that three of the samples had already been replaced by town's water, they would have to wait a little while before the other two would be replaced, but it was only a matter of time. The state of things Wt.B very satisfactory. A COMPLAINT FROM EMMY.

Mr. James Inlay, Helsby, wrote drawing attention to a—large quantity of water which lay in front of his house, and which in wet weather fllocied the interior of the premises. He asked for the evil to be remedied. Mr. Diggle said he knew that the water lodged as complained of, and he had pointed out to Mr. Yowl that the cutting of a trench would carry the water off and prevent it running on to his land, and that h 9 could help himself if he wisbed• lie had seen Mr. Youd and it appeared that he desired the building of a small wall, and if be found it was desi rabic to meet Mr. Youd in this matter he would estimate for it in the next year's estimate. Admiral Jones Parry thought it was a very undesir- able thing that a pool of water should be allowed to stand betore a man's house for generation after genera- tion. Mr. Diggle further pointed out that it would but take five minutes to cut the necessary channel which would abate the nuisance. It was decided that Mr.Diggle put one of the roadmen to do the work, and it was agreed that the Highway Sub-Committee should inspect the place and report when next they made their annual round. Death of Mr. F. J. Carlisle.

_ Our readers will hear with regret of the death of Mr Frederick Jo hn Carlisle, of Arpley-street, which occurred at his business premises at five o'clock on Tuesday afternoon. Mr. Carlisle had only been seriously ill since sunday, and had been attended by Drs. Joseph and Fox. On Tuesday morning he seemed much better, but gradually became worse and died at the time stated. Mr Carlisle was very well known throughout the town as a butcher and beerhouse keeper at Market-street, where he had been in business many years. lie was a very large property owner and ratepayer, and had a large circle of friends. The deceased leaves a widow and family of children, Mr Carlisle was identified with the Conservative party. Local Oddfellows. The monthly meeting of the Loyal Garibaldi Lodge, N. 1.0., Oddfellows was held at the Blue Lion Motel, Warrington, on Saturday. In the ab- sence of the Noble Grand (Bro Emson), P.G. Joseph W Perry took the chair and Bro John AlOdS, the vice-chair. On the proposition of P.F.G.M. J J Hands a hearty vote of welcome was accorded to Bro Herbert W Lea (volunteer) on his return from South Africa Bro Lea returned thanks, remarking how pleased he was that the lodge had kept him in benefit during his absence. After the usual business bad been disposed of, Bro Perry called upon the Grand Master of the district (Bro Edwin B Pester). to present Bro Blackshaw (retiring lodge Grand Master) with a beautifully framed past grand's cer- tificate of the order for services rendered as an official. In making the presentation Bro Pester said he was pleased to perform such an honourable duty to an able brother who had given every satisfaction to the lodge by his courtesy and interest in every- thing he had undertaken. He trusted he would still interest himself in Oddfellowship. Bro Black- shaw in returning thanks said he hod tried to do his duty and was pleased that his services were appreciated.

NOTES ON UAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

[By " Oval Ist.")

It is some satisfaction to know that Warrington are really superior to Stockport, the present wooden•spoon- ists of the Lancashire League, for recent performances have led one to imagine that tha team was one of the worst in the competition, so far as scoring powers are concerned. Stockport are much worse, however, and during their visit to Wilderspool they suffered defeat by ten points to three, notwithstanding the fact that War- ringtin were a man short for nearly half the game. Thus, the downfall of Warrington atlEdgeley Park early in the reason has been avenged, and Warrington are satisfied for the time being.

With the exception of half-back, where there will probably be some change, Warrington bad their best cup-tie team on duty, and much interest was naturally evinced in the sort of performance the men would give. To be candid, it was not very brilliant, and, perhaps it was not a fair indication of the team's abilities. Stock- portproved rather an awkward lot to shine against, for they were desperately determined to prevent any scor- ing they possibly could, in view of their critical posi- tion in the Lancashire League. Tneir spoiling tactics and good defence succeeded fairly well in effecting this object, and for some time Warrington could not make any appreciable headway. Eventually Fish man ceuvred himself into a nice position and dropped a neat goal— the first, by the way, that he has dropped for Warring- ton. This success whetted the Wanington men's appe- tite for more, and before the interval Morrison scored a grand try as the result of fine forward work, Fish add- ing a second goal. At half-time the lead for the home team was one of seven points.

The play in the second half soon indicated that War- rington were going to hold their own pretty comfort- ably, until an accident befel Rockenhall, whose shoulder was, unfortunately, di4ocated by an opponent falling upon him. As if to prove that Warrington were not greatly weakened ty the loss of Hockenhall,Figh cleverly robbed Orarn, who was waiting for a bouncing ball, and scored a fine try much to the discomfiture of the Clarets. Ten points to nil was now the total, but it was destined to be altered. Stockport had two speedy men on the left wing, and an opportunity coming their way they reduced the home team's lead by a try, W Robinson being the scorer from an opening cleverly made by Worsley. This was tho last event of the day in the scoring line and until the close play was fairly even. Just before the whistle sounded, however, Morrison had the misfortune to collide rather violently with Oram,and that player being laid out, tho referee ordered the War- rington man off the field—a decision which was far from meeting with approval. I don't think Morris3n's rough- ness was intentional and it was rather hard on him to be sent off fora fiest offence.

Reverting to W. Robinson's try, it is rather curious that this player should always score against Warrington hichever side wins. Ever since I have attended the meetings between the two clubs he has crossed the War- rington line invariably, and frequently more than once in a match. I was thinking on Saturday that the rule was going to have an exception, when Woreley made his sensational break away and Robinson scored.

Though they got the points, Warrington were u.ifor- tunate in losing llockenhall and Morrison, both of whom may be debarred from playinq in the cup-ties. It is almost certain that the former's accident will keep him out of the field for the rest of the season, and as he has been playing in excellent form of late. he will be very much missed. Hockenhall was becoming a very reliable player, and in defensive work he always shone. War- rington can ill spare him from the match with Leeds.

Other matches on Saturday resulted as expected, with one notable exception—the defeat of the competition leaders by Broughton Rangers. The latter were not at full strength, but their all-round excellence, and Swinton's rather poor form, turned the tables on the Lions. The latter failed to score a try, the winners scraping home by a try to a goal. This result, coupled with Oldham's fine victory over Runcorn, made the postponed match between Swinton and Oldham on Mon. 'day really momentous. The Lions were desperate, and it stands to their credit that they went to Oldham again and avoided defeat, the great match endicg in a draw of 3 points each. Swinton thus keep their lead, a point dividing the two teams, who have each played the same number of matches.

The Cup Ties.

Excitement has been steadily growing in Warrington during the week, and for the last two days hardly any- thing has been thought of or talked about but the chances of the," primrose and blues " for the match with Leeds at Eleadingley. The tie is an exceptionally interesting oae, from the fact that ;he winnertvill be al home until the semi-final. That victory on Saturday means a fair chance for honours will be readily seen, and it will be a determined struggle I have not tha least doubt.

Leeds lost again on their own ground on Saturday against Huddersfield, who claimed the verdict by 2 tries lip 1 goal. This places the city club fourth from the Bottom of the Yorkshire Competitien ladder, with an aggregate of only 15 points out of 27 matches. War- rington have saven clubs below them and six above them in ths Lancashire League, and have ribtained nearly a point per match,viz., 23 points out of 24 games. The records of the two clubs for the season, compared, are as follows :

Fee. itest. P. W. L. D. G. T. P. G. T. P P Wsrrington 24-10-11-3-20-26-118-18...29...123-23 Leeds

Warrington's rc ord is much the better of the two,and the figures seem to prove that the Leeds men are weak on the defetice,though fairly proficient in scoring ability. One would imagine from these facts that the visitors would have a fairly easy game, but this is not likely to be the case.

Representatives of the Warrington club who were at Leeds on Saturday give the home club in to-day's tie a good name. They have plenty of dash, and play a fair all-round game, while as regards kicking, they are ad- mittedly much superior to Warrington. If we are to win it must be be close play and combined passing, or, to be more explicit, the Lancashire style versus the Yorkshire style. The two Colemans, late of Leigh. are both playing a good game and form a rather dangerous wing pair, while Harry Varley at half, and Jagger at full back, both give an excellent account of themselves. Forward there is not thought to be much difference between Leeds and Warrington, the latter having if anything, the advantage in weight.

My own opinion is that Warrington will just about win by superior b martness behind. A lot depends on the halves and on Fish. If the passing is at all clean and smart then the left winger should make his pr.( sence felt and that is what we want. Harris, also, may distinguish himself. He has gat a lot of his old dash back.

The Warrington team was selected on Wednesday evening and is as follows :—Hallarn, full back; Fish, Isherwood, 'Dickenson and Harris, three-quarters ; R Bate and Dtickworth, half-backs ; Boardman, Morrison, Eden, Scholtze, Fell, Edmondson, Cunningham, and Swift, forwards.

Two changes are noticeable from the team which did duty last Saturday. Dickenson takes the place of Ilockenhall, and J T Bate is dropped in preference to his brother. The committee have taken the correct course here, for I don't think '• Smack " ever played a worse game in his life than last Saturlay. Ile seemed totally out of condition and dead off form. Duckworth showed all his old smartness, and locked a trifle heaver, if any- thing. " Bob" Bate has been suffering from a Sore knee, but is pronounced fit again, and he is certainly playing better football than his brother. THE WARRINGTON 1 SOCIETY. I

Annual Meeting and Dinner at the Old Academy.

Mr. F. Monks, J.P., presided over the annual meeting of the Warrington Society which was held in the Old AcadernysoniThursday last week. There were also present :—Messrs J A Ewarc, J Fairhurst, C Madeley, E 0 Osborne, H S Page, T May, J El Smethbrst, jun., M Ashton, W U J Jenkins, J Fulton Smith, E Hart, with A Bennett and EJ Hall (hon. secretaries). LE T TER FROM THE PRESIDENT. Mr. WILLIAM LONG, the president of the Society, wrote to Mr. Bennett, as follows, from Cleabarrow, Windermere :—" I see that our annual :meeting is called for to•morrow evening, and it would have given me much pleasure to attend it and dine with my fellow members after. But the Fates are against me, and I must be content to hope that you miv have a good report of past doings and a satisfactory balance 'sheet to lay before the meeting, and hearing that the Society is justifying its existence and filling a useful place in the town. Certainly we could have done clothing to regret when we rescued the historic old building from destruc- tion and possibly worse desecration to more ignoble uses. Wishing you and all a very pleasant social evening, believe me, your sincerely. Wm Losa " ALTERATION OF RULE.

It was decided on the motion of Mr May, seconded by Mr FAIRHURST, that rule 7 be altered to the effect that "subject to the approval of the executive, the • society in general meeting may elect any person distinguished in science. literature and art, as an honorary member."

Executive has not been responded to as generously as they had hoped,and further contributions are invited in order that this long-neglected and important work may be completed without delay. The ancient landmarks are rapidly disappearing. In many other places each tablets have been provided, and it is needless to emphasize the distinction they would lea I to a town whose associaticms are second to those ef scarcely any in the Kingdom. In this connection, we note with pleasure that the Town Council are beginning to give the names of old " Warrington Worthies "to appropriate local thoronghfares. Amongst the resignations which we have had to lament during the year is that of the Rev:VV H Drummond, B A, (son of Principal Drummond, of Manchester College, Oxford), who has been appointed minister of All Souls' Church, Belfast ; and it is with profound regret that we record the death of Danis D ivies, whose genial personality, dramatic skill, and literary and artistic taste had en- deared him not only to every member of the Society, but to a wide circle of friends and admirers all over the country. We are glad to state that the first fruits of his genius have been collected in a volume which will always have an honoured corner in our library. In con- clusion, we may say that every effort i 3 being made to promate that spirit of intellectual camaraderie which adds so much to the amenities of tile In an industrial centre ; and we cordially invite the active assistance of all our membars in carrying out the aims of the Society, and shall be glad to welolme any to its ranks who are genuinely interested in the town, and wish to keep alive in it the love of art and letters. The statement of accounts showed that the income received from rents, subscriptions &c., amounted to £lOO Os Bii. The expenditure totalled £127 is leavingAn adverse balance of £27 Os 41. On tho motion of the CHAIRMAN seconded by Mr Pam- rwasy.the report and balance sheet were adooted,several suggestions as to bow the income of the Society might be increased, being considered

whilst Mr Thomas May gave "The Lancashire Witches,', which was replied to by the Chairman. Mr. GEORGE VENN said he had much pleasure in pro- posing the toast of " The Good Old Town," and he felt sure no society would receive the toast more loyally than the Warrington Society. It was one of their char- acteristics to be loyal to the good old town and also to their country. (Hear, hear.) At the end of the eight teenth century and the beginning of the last century a wave of patriotism was passing over the country similar to the one they had been experiencing during the past eighteen months, and at that time the good old town was not slow in showing its sympathy. He thought it was net gener- ally known at that time that a Society similar to their society existed. Thera was then in ex- istence a club which was termed the "Amicable Club," its members consisting of the leading tradespeople of , the town, professional men, and the gentry of the neigh- beurhood. The Club was started in the usual way by subscriptions, and fines for various offeaces were levied upon the members. Then there was a 'Denied° society, called "The Bluebacks," both being in existence at the same time and having their headquarters at the seine place and consisting of the same members, The patrio- tism which existed then was apparent even now, and stirring events of the past 12 or 18 months were very similar. The toast was heartily drnnk. MC FAIRHURST in eeply, said he was one of those who felt very proud of the good old town. Fle thought he was about the tenth generation of the family in War rington. He could certainly call himself a Warring- tonian and he could remember very many changes in the town. He found that during his time the area of the borough had increased more than twice what it vvas 40 toirmseos tit er snaugmo e rTohf hpooupsue lart tbiyo no ehaardl yi ret chrreeaes teidmbeys t ah nr ede the rateable value about four times. Ile had even beard it said that the times were much better 40 or 50 years ago, and he had been told they had not so many ratea—(lattehter)—but he thought that was not quite true. Forty years ago they were paying rates amounting to Sc 103 in the pound, and that amount was the same now. The debt at that time was £20,000 and now it was £650,000. They had gone on paying and wiping off some of those debts. He was 'resent at one of the meetings of Municipal Corpora- tions, when he heard a statesman Say that a great deal had been said of the increase of debts of Corpora- tions, lie else stated that the debts of the Corporations were nearly twice the amount of the national debt, and went on to say that he danything of that because his experience wasid more the debts of a town the more they not thinkthe wore preparing to put themselves in readiness for a future. That etates-nan went on to say " show me a town that had no debts, and I will show you a town that is going to decay." For their £650,000 they had many valuable properties—gas works, waterworks, and other things. Without attempting to be a prophet he thought they would have a good time in Warrington. He was still of opinion despite the aspersions cast upon them during the last few weeks that they wodld come out of it clear. During the time he had been corm cted with the Corporation he had seen a change in every member of the Council, and he believed nosy that they , had a very good Council. (Hear, bear). A Connell that I was preparing to do all that was necessary for progress and he believed that given a fair chance they would be- I come one of the principal towns of Lancashire. (Hear, I hear). In looking over the minutes and documents at the Town Hall he ascertained that at one time the Cor- poration held neatly all the land from Corporation-street to Market-gate and where the market now stands. S me of that land was sold at 5s per yard,. and if they could now buy it at twenty times that price they would buy to-morrow. (Hear, hear, and laughter). Ile had always tried to uphold the good name, and he would like to see teings progressing—perhaps a little quicker than they *ere now' What hey wanted were the people most Interested to come and do their share. (dear, hear). Mr. E. J. flux proposed the health of " The Chair man." Most of them knew how deeply he had laid his impress on the town for its advanoernenamt and benefit. Toe Chairman was one of the most familiar figures amonest them—(hear, hesl)—having lived amongst nem all his life. He had great pleasure in proposing the toast.

The toast was drunk with musical honours. The Curapuarr expressed his thanks, and said he had lived amongst them and intended to live amongst them. Warrington was the birthplace of his parents, and a few miles away was the home of his ancestors. Lin thanked them sincerely for so heartily drinking his health. Mr SEGAR OWEN gave the " Warrington Society," the object of which he said was for the advancement of hobble; which they were able to take up after their day's work, and also that they might be brought to gether to discuss congenial matters and learn from one another. He coupled with it the name of Ildr Bennett, because he could not conceive any other man who had ,tried harder tu further any society that had aSitS

ANNUAL REPORt AND BALANCE B.EIEEr.

The SECRETARIES presented their annuallrepert and statement of accounts. The report read as follows

----- We are able to look back upon the year which has jest ended with a good deal of satisf scion. Our membership has been fully maintained, and those who have been in the habit of trequenting tho Old Academy and actively identifying themselves with the work of the Society have become increasingly attached to both. The objects whicio the founders had in view in:preserving the one and establishing the other are more and more appreciated, and the Academy is gradus,lly winning recognition as the meeting-place for what is best and most enlightened in the town. The appearance of the building has been much. improved by the planting of additional trees and Ivy, and the erection of a flower-box above the front door ; arid the collection of local pictures, hooks, and relics is steadily getting larger. Amongst the additions which have been made Co these in the course of the year we wish to specially refer to a complete set of John Howard's " Prison" works, all first editions,and printed by William Evros, which has been kindly pretented by Mr John Robert Carter, P.S.S.,EIon Librarian to the South Place Ethical Soci-ity, London. As we pointed out last year, old Warrinvon books or pictures are paculiarly acceptable, and the Executive ate always glad to have an opportunity of acquiring them either by gift, loan or percnase. The dining and smoke rooms have become extremely p opular. though we regret that the " Smokers' and Talkers' Nights " which were started last winter have had to be dis- continued through the want of proper support. The library is slowly growing, the book club flourishes, and the " Club" nights have again been most successful. The first aanual dinner was well attended, and was in every way a v irsz pleasing function The same applies to the picnic to D dainere, in titigual', of which a photo• graphic souvenir has been presented to the Society by Mr Walter Crompton. A subsequent cycle picnic to Plumb- ley was also much enjoyed ; and similar picnics, it is hoped, will be arranged, at frequent intervals, to other interesting places, and especially no those with historical associations. The Polyglot and Philatelie societies have once more made their home at the Ac,demy, and the attention of similar Societies is invited to the accom- modation which it is able to offer. INDIGESTION .9 BILIOUSNESS

Ended by Charles Forde's BILE lugs.

(Extract from the "Cheadle Herald"). R.._, N example of the power of Charles Forde's BILE BEANS for Biliousness in curing chronic easel of Indigestion and Liver Troubles, has recently been brought to light through the instrumentalit: of the "Cheadle Herald;" the subject being Mrs. Hannah Whitehurst, of Plant St- Cheadle, Staffordshire. When the "Herald" representative called upon her, he found her in perfect health, and she very kindly detailed to him the following facts :—" Somewhere about nine years ago, I began to suffer from severe attacks of biliousness and indiges- tion. I had to be exceedingly careful as to what I took, but notwith- standing all the care I exercised, after each meal I experienced great pain in the chest and back, and still more severely between the shoulders. I hardly ever got up in the morning without the headache, which would continue throughout the day. Naturally I obtained medical advice, and for a long time was constantly under the care of the doctor. His medicine, however, did no more than afford me a temporary relief, and I spent pounds without obtaining any material benefit. This miserable state of affairs continued until last September. In that month a pamphlet was put under my door. and, as much out of curiosity as anything else, I r?ad it. To my surprise I found it stated how a number of eases similar to my own had been cured by use of Charles Forde's Bile Beans for Biliousness; and I was so impressed with some ol the eases that I decided to try the Beans. I procured a box without delay, and began to take them regularly. To my. delight before the first box was empty my headache disappeared. By degrees the biliousness also ceased to trouble me, and as I continued with the Beans, my appetite improved, and .once more I began to feel well. Of course, finding that to be the ease, I obtained a further supply, and as a result, lam now as well and strong as ever I was in my life. Ino longer feel weary and fagged in the morning as I used to do, and I take a pleasure in performing my household duties. Bile Beans alone have brought about this cure, and I have so much faith in them that I shall always keep them in my house. I have recommended them to several of my neighbours and friends, and shall continue to do so, wherever I lind anyone suffering as I did."

NOTHING PERSONAL INTENDED. —The family was to have the luxury of a private telephone. The battery and wiring had been put in, and all that was necessary was the establishment of the connections. The handsome daughter of the housellold waited for the service to begin. The weloome ring came at a time when she was the only member of the family at home. She rushed to the telephone and gave the answering "Hello !" "Hello !" came back, cheerfully. "You're 31." "What 1" exclaimed the girl, hardly able to believe her ears. "You're 31," repeated the voice. "I'm not; and don't you dare insult me. Why, I'm only just a little over--" The voice came back over the wire with a jolly laugh. "I'll have to beg your pardon, miss. I meant that 31 was your telephone number."

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY, MARCH 1 1901.

THE WAR.

LATEST TELEGRAMS.

Reported Surrender of

General Botha.

The London "Daily Chronicle," says :—"We have received from a quarter that we have every reason to believe trustworthy, a report that General Botha has surrended to Lord Kitchener. According to earlier information arrang-ments had been 'made to receive General Botha in Lord Kitchener's camp at the end of this week, but if what we now hear is true, events have evidently ripened with unexpected rapidity."

===== 0002601_19010301_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
Another Version.

Tbe " Pall Mall Gazittee " says :—lnformation has just reached amniag-street that General Louis Botha f 3rmally surrendered to L3rd Kitchener at Middelburg shortly before nine o'clock to-day (Thursday). All details had previously been arranged, and the modus roiveadi had been settled some days before.

No Information at the War Office.

The War Office says it has no confirmation of the reported surrender of Botha.

Two Runcorn Men Join the Yeomanry.

Private T Mellor, late of the Runcorn Volunteer Detachment, and dr E D Douglas, son of Mr James Douglas, 4, Lniuster-gardens, have joined the Imperial Yeomanry. Private Mellor, who is tne son of Mr William Mellor, left Runcorn for Aldershot on Monday. Mr Douglas leaves on Tuesday.

More Runcorn Men for the Front.

On Saturday afternoon, Privates Cnarlesworth, Par- kinson, Griffiths, and Clarke, who have been called up for active service in South Africa, left Runcorn Station by the 2.4 train for the Chester depot. TheP and G (Runcorn) companies of the 2nd V.B.C.R. assembled at the Drill Hall at 1.30, and escorted their comrades to the station,led by the band, under the direction of Band- master Outhwaite. Captain Timmins was in command and there were also present—Lieut. Linaker, Sergeant Instructor Callaghan. Sergeaats Harvey. Edwards, and PI cenix. The streets were lined with spectators, and a large crowd hal congregated at the approaches to the station to give the men a hearty send-off. On arrival at the station Captain Timmins read a telegram from Cap- tain Ashten, commander of the detachment, expressing regret that business in London prevented him from see- ing the men off, and conveying his best wishes for their success and safe return. Captain Timmins addressed a few words to the men, and expressed the hope that they would worthily uphold the credit of their town. The men entrained amid the loud and prolonged cheer- ing of their townsmen and to the strains of " Auld Lang Syne " by the band, These four voluhteers have been on the reserve list for over twelve months. A sum of money had been collected for the benefit of their wives and families, and it is proposed shortly to hold a concert with the object of raising funds to insure their lives while on active service. The men were plaetographed before leaving the station.

Departure of the Cheshire Volunteers.

The second body of the Volunteer Special Service Com- pany of the Cheshire Regiment sailed tram Southampton for South Africa in the as. Oratava on Monday. The company consists of 113 men, and three officers—Captain Abercrombie (in command), Lieutenant Frost, and Sub- Lieutenant Moir—and goes to relieve the Special Service ICompany now at the front. For the last three weeks the men have been located at the Chester Grand Stand, at the Roodee, where they have been steadily and patiently acquiring drills of a nature that will stand them in good stead when put to the test of practical warfare. The men have been fitted out with the customary khaki kit, and on Saturday evening were entertained to supper by the Mayor (Alderman H T Brown) at the Town Hall. The time for the departure from Chester was put at 12 15 on Sunday night. Shortly before the mm n left their temporary quarters the Mayor visited them as they were drawn up ready for the march and spoke a few words of encouragement. Notwithstanding the lateness of the hour a large crowd had assembled to witness the departure. The Watergate and the Walls were packed, as were also the streets and Rows where the Mell passed. A large crowd load collected on the station platforms, which grew denser as the men, headed by the R lie Band, approached. It was with difficulty that a way could be kept clear for the Volunteers to get to their train, but after a gcod deal of good-humoured pushing and struggling the company were safely entrained. The last good-byes were hurriedly said, the signal given, the band struck up "Auld tang syne," and amidst a volley of cheers the train glided forward, and the men were fairly on their way to the relief of their tiomrades at the front ere the station was clear of people.

A Boer Account of Spion Kop. Monday, 29, 1, 1900. Spion /Cop. The leader of the report riders wires from Veld near Ladysmith. I was yesterday on the battlefield of Spion Kop. From inform- ation received it appears that on Tuesday (23rd January), in the night, the enemy marched to the west of Spion Kop, the force consisting of Lancashire Fusiliers, the Lancasters, and half a company of engineers. At one o'clock they reached the hill and at once commenced to make entrenchments. The fire brigade of Vrijheid. 60 strong, took to flight. There was a thick fog, which lifted at about 7 a m, on Wednesday. 24th January. Our burghers meanwhile had mounted the hill,and immediately on the fog disappear- ing the fighting began on Spion Kop and lasted till Thurs- day when the enemy fled. The enemy was repeatedly reinforced until their force had increased to about 4,000 men. We were about 500 strong. It is difficult to state the enemy's loss, but it is estimated at 800 killed, whilst the number of wounded can only be guessed at. Yester- day there were still about 200 unburied. Major-General Woodgate, who commanded the brigade, was killed: The Lancashire Fusiliers were totally annihilated with the exception of 110 who were taken prisoners. Of their forty-three officers only one remains, the others being killed or wounded, amongst the latter being the commander, Lieutenant-Colonel Bloomfield. According 'to an English officer, Buller is unhurt, and he further said that be had never experienced such firing as that of the Boers. Ten of Thorneycroft's mounted infantry took up a good position behind a kraal, and, when the burghers noticed them, six of them ran to a position , from which they could fire at them, and in three minutes all ten were dead. He (the officer) disapproves of night attacks and attributes their defeat to fog They had intended to surround the fire brigade. The enemy has now again taken his waggons across the river The Chief Telegraph Department, Pretoria, reports that yesterday only a few shots were heard near_the Tugela. It seems the enemy intend retiring altogether after their fruitless efforts to get through. On one of the kopjes General Cronje (Uncle Andries) still found seventeen unburied corpses, whilst at Spion Bop there are still many bodies which the enemy evidettly do not intend Ito bury.—From "Some Boer War Bulletins," in the ' Corahill May azine ' for March
