EARLESTOWN & NEWTON.

NEWTON URI3A.N DISTRICT COUNCIL.

A spacial meeting was held at the Town Hall. Earlestown on Monday afternoon for the purpose of making selections from the candidates for gas and water engineer, and gas works clerk. For the post of engineer there were 37 candidates.which number was reduced to 4,and these appeared before the com- mittee on Monday, one being ultimately selected for recommendation to the Council. For the posi- tion of gas works clerk, there were 117 applica- tions, which were reduced to 9 and these appeared before the committee. The choice ultimately fell upon Mr Thomas Swift of Earleatown, now em- ployed by the Sankey Sugar Co, and he will be recommended to the Council on Monday for appoint- ment.

THE PROPOSED COTTAGE HOSPITAL FOR EARLES-

TOWN AND NEWTON.

The first meeting of the committee formed for; purpose of carrs log througo this project was hold on Tuesday evening in the r)wn Hall, Earlestowo. and some advancement was made. It was decided in the first place, to work for the provision of hospital to accommodate 20 bads, at an estimated cost of .25,000, and that the hospital should be known as the " Newton-in-Makerfield Victoria Memorial Cottage Hospital." Various suggestions were brought forward and discussed as to the best means of raising the r squisite amount of money but the one most in favour was that of raising it by means ot weekly subscriptions of a penny or a half- penny from all the employs engaged at the various works in the district, and 'ey donations from em- ployers,and others. It was also decided that meet- ings of the emplos es at the different works be held at which the project would be explained and repre- sentatives invited to join the committee. The pro- ceedings were quite enthusiastic, and no fear was expressed as to the required amount being raised in the course of a couple of years.

SUICIDE AT HAYDOCK. A determined case ot s 'idle took place at Hay- dock an Sunday morning. About a quarter past nine, Toornas Pimblett, engine winder at the Old Boston Collieries, whilst in the engine house saw a man jump into Haydock badge reeervoir, ahout quarter of a mile away. He gave an alarm, and later on the body was recovered, and proved to be that of Martin Dowd. collier, age 31, who lodged at Barber's-square, Ashton. The body was removed to the Run's Head Hotel, to await an inquest. —The inquest was held at the Ram's Head, Hay- dock, on Tuesday.—Patrick Dowd sail that at half- past-one on Sunday morning he WAS going home when he met his Irother who was under the influence of drink. He took him home with him, and then want to bed leaving his brother asleep on the mat in front of the fire. He never had any suspicion that Martin would take away his life.— Sarah Dowd said that when she went downstairs in the morning she saw Martin there and roused him. He bade her "good morning, " and went out. She did not esnsider him strange in his manner.— Thomas Pimblett, engine winder at the Old Bolton Colliery said that on Sunday he Paw 31 trtin Dowd divest himself of his cap, coat and waistcoat and jump into a reservoir. When he and another man reached the spot the man had disappeared. The Coroner having summed up, the jury returned a verdict to the effect that deceased deliberately and feloniously killed himself. VOLUNTEER SMOKING CONCERT AT NEWTON. On Saturday evening one of the best Smoking Concerts ever held under the auspices of the sixth V. 13, Kings Lancashire Regiment, Newton Detach- ment, took place at the Legh Arms Hotel. There would be about 150 present. The chair was occu- pied by Sergeant Instruc.;or Hancock who was supported by Colour Sergeant Appleton, Sergeant Major Pearce and Regimental Sergeant Major Strange (Lancashire Hussars). One of the principal features was the gramaphonic selections by the instrument belonging to Mr Owen of the Farm School. Among the vocal items those by Messrs Graves 'and Hayward called for special com- ment as the contributors displayed superior vocal qualities, and their treatment of the songs rendered was worthy of great praise. The other artistes also gave excellent treats. The programme was as follows:—" Rocked in the cradle of the deep" Private Davereux ; Gramaphone "Queen oc the Earth"; Song "What do you think of the Irish now," C Penile- bury; song" Boys of the Old Brigade," Sergeant Wright; duet, "Larboard Watch," Troopers Bartley and Newell; song "Monarch of the woods," Mr Hayward; song" Drum-Major of the band," Private Ryan; song. EXHIBITION AT ‘VARRINGTON.

Novel Attraction at the Parr Hall.

An exhibition of considerable interest was opened in the Parr Hall, Warrington on Tuesday afternoon, and will continue until March Bth. By means of this attractive exhibition a syndicate of the leading manufacturers of household requisites are seeking to bring their specialides to the direct notice of the public. The 3shibition is undoubtedly a fine advertisement, and it will no doubt be attended- with as complete a success as has been the case &se where. It has now been in existence four years, and many imp irtant centres have been visited. The organization and management of the exhibition under the superintendence of Mr. W. Goodall, the manager, are excellent, and judging from the class of the exhibits and the variety of interests raprasented, Parr Hall will attract thousands of visitors during the exhibition. The arrange- ment of the exhibits has been on somewhat similar lines to those adopted at the trades exhibitions in previPus years, and though more of a genatal than local character will prove equally interesting. Such well-known firms as Messrs Richmond and Com- pany, the Warrington Electric Lighting Company, Bovril Limited, esirs Van Route a, Messrs Br own and Poison, and others, are among the exhibitors. The promoters claim by the exhibition to practically dem instrate the ad vantages of using up-to•date fo preparations, domestic and sanitary appliances and labour-saving and scientific inventions. Each after- noon and evening Miss Smallwood, of the firm of Selessni R3chmond and Company, Limited, of this town, gives cookery demonstrations an I lectures. The Empire Orchestra play selections of music each afternoon and evening. There are several other miner attractions which lend additional interest to the exhibition. There have been large attendances each afternoon and evening since the exhibition was opened, and as no charge is made for admission, Parr Hall is sure to be a centre of attraction next week. Children's Botanical Competition. A short time ago a series of three object lessons in Plant Natural History were exhibited in the botanical gallery at the Museum. Prizes to the value of 53, 33, And 23, were kindly offered by Mr Linnmus Greenibg (chairman of the Museum Sub. Committee). Questions to be answered were pub- lished with each lesson, and considerable excitement prevailed among the children during the first week of exhibition. More than a hundred actually took away copies of the questions, and on one occasion there promised to be a lively competition between the children from various schools. Many competi- tors lost marks through not sending in answers to all three sets of questions, but the answers of the three prize-winnners were very satisfactory. The third on the list unfortunately lost marks for not Sending in a complete sot of the rimple specimens asked for, altho,lgh these specimens (twigs of trees', a pea-seedling, &c.), were within the reach of all. rho names of the prize-winners are as follows : First, Lavinia Hewitt; second, Jessie Smallwood; third, Rose Tynan. Among the other competitors Alfred Allender, and Gertrude Fisher deserve praise. but they only sent in answers to one set of questions

The Union was called "the Workhouse" so far as regarded its buildings and general --egla establishment. It was intended that every pauper so far as in him or her lay should earn his or her own living, and that was done in various ,ways. The able- bodied were put on to regular work and to a great . extent did earn their own living, but there were a large n•—nhnr nf partially or totally 'incapable " people Illatto e were put to work of a lighter nature : —es officers of the Union ; and then there came those who could not do anything in the ordinary way directly for the use of the Workhouse. Ie was, there- fore, suggested some years ago that these infirm people, these convalescents, should be asked to do something light with their fingers for the double purpose of passing the time and of Useling that they were of some use to I society. They did not want to put the idea into a pauper's mind that he was no use and was thrown on COO rates. Ever pauper went to the Workhouse with a terribly bitter seeee of disappointment at his heart, and their obj-ct was to raise the hope .f all of them, There- fore opportunities were given to them in some way to pay back to the Guardians the cost of their keep, and that was also a great help towards the recovery of those who Were sick in hospital. (Hear, hear ) In speaking of the new dietary tables for Workhouse inmates, the Chairman remarked that something might perhaps be done to lessen the rigour of the law with reference to what might be taken into the Workhouse. He regretted to hear of Mrs EJeleten's forthcoming retirement from the Board, but he a as very pleased to learn that she did not intend at prelent ,to give up her position as secretary to the ' Brabazon society. The last time she (the Mayoress) was in the Infirmary she was struck More than ever by the value of the Brabazm work to the inmates. Not long ago she saw thp excite- ment of some of the patients about what they were do- ing. BREACH OF TUB SABBATH AT WIDNES. At the Whines Patty Session on Thursday morn- ing, John Lytbgoe, ehopkeeper, Mersey-road, was charged with exposing goods for sale on Sunday. The R Rev Corlett,curate,soid be visited the shop on two occasions, and sow lads consuming icecream. He reported the matter to the police, and pronged- ings were taken. Mr Knowles defended, and spoke strongly on the case as being trivial, and if upheld, detrimental to the interests enjoyed by working- men. The case was dismissed.

objects literature and the advancement of the town. (Hear, hear). Mr BENNETT acknowledged the compliment in suitable terms, and said he thought they had every reason for encouragement in the Society which had done much to vindicate its existence. After the work it had done they in the town ought to appreciate it and not disparage it. He alluded to the distinguished men who had been produced in Warrington—Luke Fildes, Henry Woods and several others—and the School of Art was prockcing men who followed in the steps of those famous men, and who wanted to revive the love of science art and letters. It was no mean thing to belong to a town of many industries, which produced so many useful and important articles known all over the world. He -hoped the members would do their best to make the Society grow. (Hear, hear.) During the evening songs and recitations were given by some of the members, and the gathering closed with the singing of the National Anthem. LOCAL COUNTY COUNCIL ELECTION. Mr Pemberton Too Late. Mr J Charlton Parr, JP, the retiring county ' councillor for the Appleton division, bas been re- elected without opposition. The latest hour for receiving nominations was five o'clock on Friday. At that time no other nomination paper had been banded in, but within a minute afterwards a paper was presented on behalf of Mr John Pember- ton, of Stockton Heath. The returning officer held that the conditions had not been complied with and declared the nomination invalid. Mr Parr was therefore returned unopposed In the first paper Mr Parr was proposed by Alfred Jaine,seconded by Frederick Rose, and assented to by Albert Moss, Edward Reddish, Charles Hallows, Jeremiah Drury, Thomas Pierpoint, James fleas, H Beresford Melville and John Fairhurst. In a second paper he was proposed by William Long, seconded by Alan Lister-Kaye and assented, to by Richard Boardman, Joseph James Fairh urst Samuel Massey Houghton, Henry S Leyland William Owen, F A Frost, Arthur Pigot and Henry Milling.
